Full Γ − \Gamma- expansion for the level-two large deviation rate functionals of non-reversible one-dimensional diffusions with periodic boundary conditions
Claudio Landim, and Christian Maura

Abstract
. Consider the diffusion process d ​ X ϵ ​ ( t ) = b ​ ( X ϵ ​ ( t ) ) ​ d ​ t + 2 ​ ϵ ​ a ​ ( X ϵ ​ ( t ) ) ​ d ​ W t , dX_{\epsilon}(t)={\textsf{b}}(X_{\epsilon}(t))\,dt+\sqrt{2\,\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}(X_{\epsilon}(t))}\,dW_{t}, on the one-dimensional torus 𝕋 = [ 0 , 1 ) {\mathbb{T}}=[0,1) . Here ϵ \epsilon is the temperature, W t W_{t} a Brownian motion on 𝕋 {\mathbb{T}} and a , b functions of class C 2 ​ ( 𝕋 ) C^{2}({\mathbb{T}}) satisfying further conditions. Denote by P ​ ( 𝕋 ) {\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}}) the set of probability measures on 𝕋 {\mathbb{T}} equipped with the weak topology, and by ℐ ϵ : P ( 𝕋 ) → [ 0 , + ∞ ) {\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}\colon{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})\to[0,+\infty) the level two large deviation rate functional of the diffusion X ϵ ​ ( ⋅ ) X_{\epsilon}(\cdot) . We derive a full Γ − \Gamma- expansion of ℐ ϵ {\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon} , as ϵ → 0 \epsilon\to 0 , expressing it as ℐ ϵ = 1 ϵ ​ 𝒥 ( − 1 ) + 𝒥 ( 0 ) + ∑ p = 1 𝔮 ^ 1 θ ϵ ( p ) ​ 𝒥 ( p ) , {\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\;{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}\;+\;{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}\;+\;\sum_{p=1}^{\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}}\frac{1}{\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}}\;{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}\,, where 𝒥 ( − 1 ) {\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)} , 𝒥 ( 0 ) {\mathscr{J}}^{(0)} , 𝒥 ( p ) : P ​ ( 𝕋 ) → [ 0 , + ∞ ] {\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}\colon{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})\to[0,+\infty] represent rate functionals, independent of ϵ \epsilon , and θ ϵ ( p ) \theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon} are the time-scales at which the Markov process X ϵ ​ ( ⋅ ) X_{\epsilon}(\cdot) exhibits a metastable behaviour.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary
60J60,
60F10,
Secondary
60H10

1.
Introduction

The metastable behavior of Markov processes has attracted considerable
attention in recent years
[
34
,
8
,
19
]
. In
[
1
,
2
]
, the authors proposed characterizing such processes as those
that can be effectively approximated by simpler Markov chains,
typically defined on finite state spaces
[
4
]
. This approach,
based on analyzing the trace of the process within the wells, has
since been successfully applied to a wide range of models in
statistical mechanics and diffusion processes
[
3
,
7
,
29
,
18
,
17
]
. This line of research culminated in

[
25
]
, which established that a Markov process is metastable in
the above sense if, and only if, the solutions to the associated
resolvent equations are asymptotically constant within the wells.
This property was proved for non-reversible diffusion processes

[
31
,
22
,
23
,
27
]
, for zero range dynamics
[
33
,
26
]
, and for symmetric inclusion dynamics
[
16
]
.

Let
x_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
be the diffusion process

dx_{\epsilon}(t)\;=\;-\,\nabla U(x_{\epsilon}(t))\,dt\;+\;\sqrt{2\,\epsilon}\,dW(t)\;,

where
\epsilon
stands for the temperature,

U\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
a Morse potential, and
W(\cdot)
a

d
-dimensional Brownian motion. Denote by
I_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
the
associated (Donsker and Varadhan) level two large deviations rate
functional
[
9
]
.

Assuming the genericity condition that each well has a distinct depth,
Di Gesù and Mariani
[
13
]
proved that the rate functional

I_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
converges, in the sense of
\Gamma
-convergence
– the natural notion of convergence for rate functionals

[
32
]
. More precisely, they showed that there exist rate
functionals
J_{-1}
,
J_{0}
,
J_{p}\colon{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,

1\leq p<{\mathfrak{q}}_{0}
such that

\Gamma-\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon\,I_{\epsilon}\,=\,J_{-1}\,,\quad\Gamma-\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}I_{\epsilon}\,=\,J_{0}\,,\quad\Gamma-\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}I_{\epsilon}\,=\,J_{p}\,.

(1.1)

Here,
{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
denotes the space of probability measures on

{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
endowed with the weak topology;
{\mathfrak{q}}_{0}
is the number of
local minima of the potential
U(\cdot)
;

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}=e^{h_{p}/\epsilon}
, where
h_{p}
represents the
depth of the
p
th shallowest well; and

\Gamma-\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}a_{\epsilon}I_{\epsilon}=J
means that
the sequence of rate functionals
a_{\epsilon}I_{\epsilon}

\Gamma
-
converges to
J
.

Note that
a_{\epsilon}I_{\epsilon}
represents the level two large
deviations rate functional associated with the diffusion process

x_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
accelerated by the factor
a_{\epsilon}
, that is,
the process
x_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
observed on the time-scale

a_{\epsilon}
. The preceding result therefore characterizes the
asymptotic behavior of the rate functional across
{\mathfrak{q}}+1
distinct
time-scales.

The time-scale
1
corresponds to the regime in which the diffusion
escapes from unstable stationary points (such as saddle points or
local maxima of the potential
U(\cdot)
, for instance), while

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}
represents the time-scale at which the process

x_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
starting from a well of depth
h_{p}
transitions
to a deeper well.

The assumption that each well has a distinct depth was removed in

[
21
]
, following the works
[
20
,
6
]
, which established
the
\Gamma
-convergence of the level-two large deviations rate
functional for finite-state Markov chains, and
[
28
]
, which
investigated the analogous problem for reversible random walks in a
potential field. These results were further extended in
[
15
]
to
the large deviations rate functional of the pair empirical measure and
current, as originally derived by Bertini, Faggionato, and Gabrielli
in
[
5
]
.

While the proof in
[
13
]
is purely analytical, relying on a
spectral analysis of the generator, the approaches developed in

[
20
,
6
,
28
,
21
]
are probabilistic in nature and
exploit the metastable behavior of the underlying processes.

According to
[
15
, Corollary 5.3]
, the generator of a reversible
Markov chain on a finite state space can be reconstructed from its
large deviations rate functional. Consequently, the
\Gamma
-expansion
presented in (
1.1
) not only identifies the time scales at which
metastable behavior arises, but also determines the dynamics of the
reduced Markov chain, provided it is a reversible finite-state
process.

In this article, we investigate the
\Gamma
-expansion of the level
two large deviations rate-functional for non-reversible
one-dimensional diffusions with periodic boundary conditions. The
main result, Theorem
2.7
, provides the full
\Gamma
-expansion
for this functional and states that:

•

The shortest time scale,
\epsilon
, yields a functional

{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}
that penalizes measures not concentrated on critical
points of the drift.

•

The next scale,
1
, yields
{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}
which
assigns positive cost to measures charging unstable critical points.

•

The metastable scales
\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}
produce
functionals
{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}
that are lifted versions of the level-two
rate functionals of finite-state reduced Markov chains

\widehat{\mathbb{X}}_{p}
.

The proof combines analytic and probabilistic techniques. We use a
variational representation of the rate functional to derive lower
bounds, and we construct explicit recovery sequences using solutions
of resolvent equations and localized Gibbs measures to obtain upper
bounds. A key ingredient is the resolvent characterization of
metastability
[
25
]
, which allows us to relate the behavior of
the rate functional at scale
\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}
to the
generator of the reduced chain
\widehat{\mathbb{X}}_{p}
.

The main novelty of our work is that we do not assume the process to
be reversible. To our knowledge, with the exception of
[
20
,
15
]
,
where non-reversible finite-state Markov chains are considered, this is
the first proof of
\Gamma
-convergence for a non-reversible dynamics.

This non-reversibility introduces significant technical challenges:
while for reversible dynamics the large deviations rate functional
admits an explicit expression, in the non-reversible setting it is
defined only through a variational formulation. The absence of an
explicit formula explains why previous studies have generally
restricted attention to reversible dynamics.

Furthermore, the method to prove the
\Gamma-\limsup
differs completely
in the reversible and non-reversible cases. In the reversible case,
see Section
7
and
8
, the recovery sequence of a
measure
\mu
concentrated on a set of local minima
m
, is
constructed by fixing the values at
m
and extending it harmonically
to the entire space. The procedure is entirely different in the
non-reversible case, and this is the main message of this article. In
the non-reversible case, see Section
9
, one has to proceed as
follows. Fix a measure
\mu
concentrated on a set
{\mathcal{M}}
of local
minima
m
. Find the tilting of the reduced Markov chain jump rates to
turn the measure
\mu
typical for the reduced dynamics. Extend the
tilting harmonically to the entire space. Consider the diffusion
tilted by this extension and its associated stationary state. Prove
that this
\epsilon
-sequence of stationary states for the tilted
dynamics is a recovery sequence of the original measure. This is
carried out in Section
9
in the last metastable
time-scale, the only one that gives rises to a non-reversible reduced
dynamics

The main challenge in the area of metastable diffusions is to
establish the metastable behavior of a diffusion process in dimension

d\geq 2
when the stationary distribution is not explicitly
known. Existing approaches to proving metastability crucially depend
on explicit computations involving the stationary state. In contrast,
the large deviations rate functional is available under weak
assumptions and does not require such explicit knowledge. This
suggests the possibility of developing analytical methods to study the
asymptotic behavior of this functional, thereby recovering the
metastable dynamics of the system.

In this direction, it may be particularly fruitful to investigate the
asymptotic behavior of the joint current–empirical measure large
deviations rate functional, which has the notable advantage of being
explicit even in the non-reversible case, unlike the level-
2
large
deviations rate functional, which is defined only through a
variational characterization.

2.
Notation and Results

In this section, we introduce the model, and state the main results.

The diffusion process.

Let
{\mathbb{T}}=[0,1)
be the one-dimensional torus of length
1
.
Consider the one-dimensional diffusion processes with periodic
boundary conditions,

dX_{\epsilon}(t)\;=\;{\textsf{b}}(X_{\epsilon}(t))\,dt\;+\;\sqrt{2\,\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}(X_{\epsilon}(t))}\,dW_{t}\;,

(2.1)

where
\epsilon>0
is the temperature, and
W_{t}
a Brownian motion on
the torus
{\mathbb{T}}
. Throughout this article, we assume that the
following conditions are fulfilled. Denote by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C({\mathbb{T}})}
the
space of continuous functions
F\colon{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, and by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C^{p}({\mathbb{T}})}
,
p\geq 1
, the elements of
C({\mathbb{T}})
with
p

continuous derivatives.

Condition H:
The drift

{\textsf{b}}\colon{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is of class
C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
. It vanishes
at a finite number of points represented by
x_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq 2N
, and

{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(x_{j})\neq 0
. The diffusion coefficient

{\textsf{a}}\colon{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is of class
C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
, and is
strictly positive:
{\textsf{a}}(x)\geq c_{0}>0
.

The generator of the diffusion process (
2.1
), denoted by

{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}
, is given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}f}\;=\;{\textsf{b}}f^{\prime}\;+\;\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}\,f^{\prime\prime}\;,\quad f\in C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})\,.

Denote by
B
the integral of the quotient
{\textsf{b}}\,/\,{\textsf{a}}
over
the torus:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}B}\;:=\;\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{{\textsf{b}}(\theta)}{{\textsf{a}}(\theta)}\,d\theta\;.

(2.2)

If
B=0
, there exists a potential
U\colon{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that

{\textsf{b}}(\theta)\,/\,{\textsf{a}}(\theta)=-\,U^{\prime}(\theta)
. In this case
the stationary state of the diffusion process is given by

\pi_{\epsilon}(d\theta)=\frac{1}{Z_{\epsilon}}\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(\theta)}\exp\{-U(\theta)/\epsilon\}\,d\theta\,,

for a suitable normalization factor
Z_{\epsilon}
, and the process
is reversible with respect to this measure.

In contrast, if the integral does not vanish the process is not
reversible, and the stationary state is not a Gibbs measure.
Faggionato and Gabrielli derived the quasi-potential for this
dynamics in
[
10
]
. The metastable behavior among the deepest
traps has been investigated in
[
30
]

Without loss of generality, we assume, from now on, that
B\geq 0
.
Our analysis includes therefore the case
B\neq 0
, where the process

X_{\epsilon}(t)
is non-reversible.

Level
2
large deviations

Denote by
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
the set of probability measures
on
{\mathbb{T}}
equipped with the weak topology, and by
L_{\epsilon}(t)
the
empirical measure of the process
X_{\epsilon}(t)
defined as:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\epsilon}(t)}\;:=\;\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\delta_{\{X_{\epsilon}(s)\}}\;ds\;,\quad t>0\,,

where
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{\{x\}}
,
x\in{\mathbb{T}}
, represents the
Dirac measure supported at
\{x\}
. Thus,
L_{\epsilon}(t)
is a random
element of
{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
and
L_{\epsilon}(t)({\mathcal{A}})
,

{\mathcal{A}}\subset{\mathbb{T}}
, stands for the proportion of time the process

X_{\epsilon}(t)
stays at
{\mathcal{A}}
in the time interval
[0,t]
.

As the Markov chain
X_{\epsilon}(t)
is irreducible, by the ergodic
theorem, for any starting point
x\in{\mathbb{T}}
, as
t\to\infty
, the
empirical measure
L_{\epsilon}(t)
converges in probability to the
stationary state, denoted by
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{\epsilon}
.

Donsker and Varadhan
[
9
]
proved the associated large
deviations principle. More precisely, they showed that for any subset

A
of
{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
,

\displaystyle-\,\inf_{\mu\in A^{o}}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu)

\displaystyle\leq\;\liminf_{t\to\infty}\min_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}}\,\frac{1}{t}\,\ln{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,L_{\epsilon}(t)\in A\,\big]

\displaystyle\leq\;\limsup_{t\to\infty}\max_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}}\,\frac{1}{t}\,\ln{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,L_{\epsilon}(t)\in A\,\big]\;\leq\;-\,\inf_{\mu\in\overline{A}}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu)\;.

In this formula,
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{\!x}
,
x\in{\mathbb{T}}
,
stands for the distribution of the process
X_{\epsilon}(t)
starting
from
x
,
A^{o}
,
\overline{A}
represent the interior, closure of the
set
A
, respectively, and

{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}\colon{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})\to[0,+\infty)
is the level two
large deviations rate functional given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu)}\;:=\;\sup_{u}\,-\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{({\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}u)(x)}{u(x)}\;\mu(dx)\;,

where the supremum is performed over all strictly positive functions

u\colon{\mathbb{T}}\to(0,\infty)
in the domain of the generator.

\Gamma-
expansion

We investigate in this article the
\Gamma
-convergence of the action
functional
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
. We first recall the definition of a

\Gamma
-expansion in a general setting, and refer to
[
32
]
for
further properties of
\Gamma
-convergence and its relation to large
deviations.

Fix a Polish space
{\mathcal{X}}
and a sequence
(U_{n}:n\in{\mathbb{N}})
of
functionals on
{\mathcal{X}}
,
U_{n}\colon{\mathcal{X}}\to[0,+\infty]
. The
sequence
U_{n}
is said to
\Gamma
-converge
to the functional

U\colon{\mathcal{X}}\to[0,+\infty]
if the two following conditions are
met:

(i)

\Gamma-\liminf
.
The functional
U
is a

\Gamma-\liminf
for the sequence
U_{n}
: For each
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and each
sequence
x_{n}\to x
,

\liminf_{n\to\infty}U_{n}(x_{n})\;\geq\;U(x)\;.

(ii)

\Gamma-\limsup
.
The functional
U
is a

\Gamma-\limsup
for the sequence
U_{n}
: For each
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
there
exists a sequence
x_{n}\to x
(called the recovery sequence) such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}U_{n}(x_{n})\;\leq\;U(x)\;.

Recall from the introduction that
\theta_{\epsilon}\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}

corresponds to the large deviations rate functional of the diffusion
process
X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
seen at the time-scale
\theta_{\epsilon}
,
that is, accelerated by this quantity. For this reason, if the
functional
U_{n}
introduced above is multiplied by a constant
a_{n}
,
we interpret
a_{n}
as a time-scale.

For two positive monotone sequences
(\alpha_{n}:n\geq 1)
and

(\beta_{n}:n\geq 1)
, denote by
\alpha_{n}\prec\beta_{n}
if

\alpha_{n}/\beta_{n}\to 0
as
n\to+\infty
. In particular, we write

1\prec\alpha_{n}
, if
\alpha_{n}\to+\infty
, and
\beta_{n}\prec 1
, if

\beta_{n}\to 0
, as
n\to+\infty
.

In the context of the previous definition, suppose that there exists a
positive monotone sequence
(\alpha^{(0)}_{n}:n\geq 1)
such that

\alpha_{n}^{(0)\,}U_{n}

\Gamma-
converges to a functional
U^{(0)}
.
If the
0-
level set of the functional
U^{(0)}
is a singleton, the
exploration over time-scales larger than
\alpha_{n}^{(0)}

ends. Otherwise, we consider the
\Gamma-
convergence of the sequence

\alpha_{n}^{(1)}U_{n}
, in a time-scale
(\alpha_{n}^{(1)}:n\geq 1)

satisfying
\alpha_{n}^{(0)}\prec\alpha_{n}^{(1)}
, to distinguish the
elements in the
0
-level set of
U^{(0)}
.

Suppose, therefore, that the
0-
level set of the functional
U^{(0)}
is
not a singleton, and that there exists a sequence
\alpha_{n}^{(1)}

such that
\alpha_{n}^{(0)}\prec\alpha_{n}^{(1)}
, and

\alpha_{n}^{(1)}U_{n}

\Gamma-
converges to some functional denoted by

U^{(1)}
. It is clear that
U^{(1)}(x)
is finite only if
x
belongs
to the
0-
level set of
U^{(0)}
. We say that
\alpha_{n}^{(1)}
is the
correct speed whenever
U^{(1)}(x)
happens to be finite if
x

belongs to the
0-
level set of
U^{(0)}
. If the
0-
level set of

U^{(1)}
is a singleton, we stop exploring longer
time-scales. Otherwise, we iterate the procedure. This recursion stops
at the first step
{\mathfrak{q}}
at which we obtain a functional

U^{({\mathfrak{q}})}:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
whose
0-
level set is a singleton.

We turn to the description of the iterative procedure over shorter
time-scales. If for every positive sequence
(\alpha_{n}:n\geq 1)

satisfying
\alpha_{n}\prec\alpha_{n}^{(0)}
, the
\Gamma
-limit of

\alpha_{n}U_{n}
is
0
, this procedure stops. Otherwise, we seek for a
sequence
\alpha_{n}^{(-1)}
and a functional
U^{(-1)}:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}

such that
\alpha_{n}^{(-1)}\prec\alpha_{n}^{(0)}
,
\alpha_{n}^{(-1)}U_{n}

\Gamma-
converges to
U^{(-1)}
, and
U^{(-1)}(x)=0
if, and only if,

U(x)<+\infty
.

If
U^{(-1)}
is such that
\alpha_{n}U^{(-1)}\to 0
, for every sequence

(\alpha_{n}:n\geq 1)
such that
\alpha_{n}\prec\alpha_{n}^{(-1)}
, we
stop exploring shorter time-scales. Otherwise, we iterate the
procedure. This recursion continues until the first step
{\mathfrak{r}}
at
which we find a time-scale
\alpha_{n}^{(-{\mathfrak{r}})}
and a functional

U^{(-{\mathfrak{r}})}:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, not equal to
0
, such that

\alpha_{n}^{-{\mathfrak{r}}}U_{n}

\Gamma-
converges to
U^{-({\mathfrak{r}})}
, and

\alpha_{n}U_{n}

\Gamma
-converges to
0
, for every

\alpha_{n}\prec\alpha_{n}^{-({\mathfrak{r}})}
.

By the end of these two procedures we obtain a family of speeds and
functionals that captures the complete asymptotics of
U_{n}
. These
considerations are summarized in the following

Definition 2.1
.

Consider a non empty Polish space
{\mathcal{X}}
and a sequence of
functionals
U_{n}:{\mathcal{X}}\to[0,+\infty]
. A full
\Gamma-
expansion of

U_{n}
is given by the speeds
(\alpha^{(p)}_{n},n\geq 1)
,

-{\mathfrak{r}}\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
, and the functionals

U^{(p)}:{\mathcal{X}}\to[0,+\infty]
,
-{\mathfrak{r}}\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
, if:

(1)

The speeds
\alpha^{(-{\mathfrak{r}})}_{n},\dots,\alpha^{({\mathfrak{q}})}_{n}
are
positive sequences such that
\alpha^{(p)}_{n}\prec\alpha^{(p+1)}_{n}
,
for every
-{\mathfrak{r}}\leq p<{\mathfrak{q}}
;

(2)

\alpha_{\epsilon}^{(p)}U_{n}

\Gamma-
converges to
U^{(p)}
,

-{\mathfrak{r}}\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
;

(3)

U^{(p+1)}(x)
is finite if, and only if,
x
belongs to the

0-
level set of
U^{(p)}
, for every
-{\mathfrak{r}}\leq p<{\mathfrak{q}}
;

(4)

\beta_{n}U_{n}

\Gamma-
converges to
0
, for every sequence

\beta_{n}\prec\alpha_{n}^{(-{\mathfrak{r}})}
; and

(5)

the
0-
level set of
U^{({\mathfrak{q}})}
is a singleton.

In this case, we express the expansion as

U_{n}=\sum_{p\in-{\mathfrak{r}}}^{{\mathfrak{q}}}\frac{1}{\alpha_{n}^{(p)}}\,U^{(p)}.

In this article, we derive the full
\Gamma-
expansion of the level
two large deviation rate functional
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
. We establish the
connection of the
\Gamma
-expansion to the metastable behavior of the
diffusion
X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
.

Pre-metastable time-scales

Denote by
{\mathcal{C}}\subset{\mathbb{T}}
the set of the critical points of the ODE

\dot{x}(t)\,=\,{\textsf{b}}(x(t))\,,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{C}}}\,:=\,\{\theta\in{\mathbb{T}}:{\textsf{b}}(\theta)=0\}\;.

(2.3)

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
,
{\mathcal{W}}
be the sets of stable and unstable critical
points, respectively,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{M}}}:=\{m\in{\mathcal{C}}:-\,{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(m)>0\},\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{W}}}:=\{\sigma\in{\mathcal{C}}:-\,{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(\sigma)<0\}.

By condition H, every critical point is either stable or unstable, so
that
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{M}}\cup{\mathcal{W}}
.

For
\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
, denote by
\mu_{\rm ac}
,

\mu_{\rm s}
the absolutely continuous and the singular
part, respectively, of the measure
\mu
with respect to the Lebesgue
measure denoted by
\lambda
. Let
m_{\mu}

be the Radon-Nikodym derivative of
\mu_{\rm ac}
with respect to

\lambda
.

Let
{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}:{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})\to[0,+\infty)
be the functional
given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}(\mu)}\>:=\>\frac{1}{4}\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{{\textsf{b}}^{2}}{{\textsf{a}}}\;d\mu\;-\;\frac{1}{4}\,\frac{1}{\langle 1/({\textsf{a}}m_{\mu})\rangle}\,B^{2}\;,

(2.4)

where
B
is the constant introduced in (
2.2
). In this formula
and below, for a nonnegative function
h\colon{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\langle h\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}h(x)\,dx
. Clearly, if
1/m_{\mu}

is not Lebesgue integrable, the second term vanishes. The first term
in the
\Gamma-
expansion of
{\mathscr{J}}_{\epsilon}
is provided by the
following result.

Proposition 2.2
.

Assume that condition
(H)
is in force. Then,

\epsilon\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}

\Gamma
-converges to
{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}
. The

0
-level set of the functional
{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}
consists of all convex
combinations of Dirac measures supported on the elements of
{\mathcal{C}}
.
Moreover,
\alpha_{\epsilon}\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}

\Gamma
-converges to
0

for any positive sequence
\alpha_{\epsilon}
such that

\alpha_{\epsilon}\,\epsilon^{-1}\to 0
.

We turn to the second term in the expansion. Recall that we denote by

\delta_{\{z\}}
,
z\in{\mathbb{T}}
or
{\mathbb{R}}
, the Dirac measure supported
on
\{z\}
. Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})}
be the set of probability measures
on a metric space
{\mathbb{M}}
equipped with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra.
Let
{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}\colon{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})\to[0,+\infty]
be given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}(\mu)}\>:=\>\begin{cases}\sum_{z\in{\mathcal{C}}}\ w(z)\zeta(z),&\text{if there exists $w\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathcal{C}})$
such that}\ \mu=\sum_{z\in{\mathcal{C}}}\ w(z)\,\delta_{\{z\}}\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise}\ ,\end{cases}

(2.5)

where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\zeta(z)}:=\max\{\>{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)\,,\,0\>\}.

(2.6)

Clearly
\zeta(z)>0
if
z
is an unstable solution of the ODE
(
2.3
), and it vanishes if it is a stable solution.

The second result of the paper reads as follows. It corresponds to the
time-scale at which the diffusion process
X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
escapes
from the local maxima and provides the cost for doing so.

Proposition 2.3
.

Assume that condition (H) is in force. Then,
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}

\Gamma-
converges to the functional
{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}
given by
(
2.5
). The
0
-level set of the functional
{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}

consists of all convex combinations of Dirac measures supported on the
elements of
{\mathcal{M}}
.

Metastable time-scales

We turn to the time-scales at which the diffusion process escapes from
the stable solutions of the ODE (
2.3
). This is easier to
explain if we lift the diffusion to the line. Regard
{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)

and
{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
as periodic functions on
{\mathbb{R}}
, and denote by

S:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
the potential given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S(x)}=-\int_{0}^{x}\frac{{\textsf{b}}(z)}{{\textsf{a}}(z)}\;dz.

The function
S(\cdot)
is periodic if, and only if, the constant
B

introduced in (
2.2
) vanishes. Consider the one-dimensional
diffusion process on
{\mathbb{R}}
given by

dY_{\epsilon}(t)\;=\;{\textsf{b}}(Y_{\epsilon}(t))\,dt\;+\;\sqrt{2\,\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}(Y_{\epsilon}(t))}\,dW_{t}\;,

(2.7)

where
W_{t}
is the Brownian motion on
{\mathbb{R}}
.

First order metastable structure.
Denote by

{\mathscr{C}}
,
{\mathscr{M}}
,
{\mathscr{W}}
the lifting to
{\mathbb{R}}
of the sets
{\mathcal{C}}
,

{\mathcal{M}}
,
{\mathcal{W}}
, respectively. Label the sets
{\mathscr{M}}
and
{\mathscr{W}}
in
the following way:

{\mathscr{M}}:=\{m_{k}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}\;,\hskip 14.22636pt{\mathscr{W}}:=\{\sigma_{k}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\},

so that
m_{k+N}=m_{k}+1
and
\sigma_{k+N}=\sigma_{k}+1
, for every

k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Without loss of generality, assume that

m_{-1}<0<\sigma_{0}<m_{0}<\dots<\sigma_{N-1}<m_{N-1}<1<\sigma_{N}.

Let
{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k)
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, be the sets defined by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k)}:=\{m_{k}\}\,\;\;\text{and set}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{S}}_{1}}:=\{{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k):k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}.

(2.8)

For every
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}h^{+}_{k}}:=S(\sigma_{k+1})-S(m_{k}),\hskip 28.45274pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}h^{-}_{k}}:=S(\sigma_{k})-S(m_{k}),

which can be regarded as the escape barriers on the right and left of
the set
{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k)
, respectively. For each

k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
define the height (of the escape barrier) of

{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k)
as the minimum of its right and left escape
barriers:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}h_{k}}:=\min\{h^{-}_{k},h^{+}_{k}\}.

Clearly, since
b
and
a
are periodic,

h^{\pm}_{k+N}=h^{\pm}_{k}\;,\hskip 14.22636pth_{k+N}=h_{k}\,,\quad\forall\;k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\,.

(2.9)

Let
{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
, and

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(1)}
, be the smallest height, and the associated
time-scale, respectively:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}}=\min\{h_{k}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\},\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\theta_{\epsilon}^{(1)}}=e^{{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}/\epsilon}.

(2.10)

By (
2.9
),
{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}>0
. Thus,

\theta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}\uparrow+\infty
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

For
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{1}(k)}:={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{1}(\{m_{k}\})}\,:=\,\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(m_{k})}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{S^{\prime\prime}(m_{k})}}\;,\;\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{1}(k,k+1)}\,:=\,\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{-\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k+1})}}\,.

(2.11)

Here, the indices
k
,
k+1
of
\sigma_{1}(k,k+1)
indicate that we
consider the
S
-local maximum between the local minima
m_{k}
and

m_{k+1}
. Let
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{1}(k+1,k)=\sigma_{1}(k,k+1)
.
Define the jump rates

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}R_{1}({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k\pm 1))}\;:=\;\frac{1}{\pi_{1}(k)\,\sigma_{1}(k,k\pm 1)}\;{\mathbf{1}}\{h^{\pm}_{k}={\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\}\;.

(2.12)

Therefore, the jump rate from
{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k)
to
{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k\pm 1)

vanishes if
h^{\pm}_{k}>{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
. [By definition

{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\leq h^{\pm}_{j}
for all
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.] Recall the definition
of the set
{\mathscr{S}}_{1}
given in (
2.8
). Denote by

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{X}}_{1}(\cdot)
the
{\mathscr{S}}_{1}
-valued Markov
chain induced by the generator
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}
defined as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({\mathbb{L}}_{1}f)({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k))}\,:=\,\sum_{a=\pm 1}R_{1}({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k+a))\,\big[\,f({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k+a))-f({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k))\,\big]\;,\quad k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\;.

As
{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
,
{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)
are periodic functions, the jump
rates defined in (
2.12
) are periodic as well:

R_{1}({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k+N),{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k+N\pm 1))=R_{1}({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k\pm 1))
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Therefore, if

\Pi\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{T}}
represents the projection defined by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Pi(x)}=x-\lfloor x\rfloor\,,\quad\text{where
$\lfloor r\rfloor$ is the integer part of a real number $r$}\,,

(2.13)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{1}(\cdot)}:=\Pi({\mathbb{X}}_{1}(\cdot))
is a
continuous-time Markov chain taking values in

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{S}}_{1}}:=\{{\mathcal{M}}_{1}(k);0\leq k<N\}
, where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{M}}_{1}(k)}=\{{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}\}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}}:=\Pi(m_{k})
. Denote
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{1}}
the generator of the projected Markov
chain
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{1}(\cdot)
, and let

{\mathbb{I}}^{(1)}\colon{\textsf{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{1})\to[0,+\infty)
be the associated
large deviations rate functional:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{I}}^{(1)}(\mu)}\,:=\,\sup_{H}-\,\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}}\frac{(\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{1}H)({\mathcal{M}})}{H({\mathcal{M}})}\mu({\mathcal{M}})\,,

where the supremum is performed over all positive functions

H\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Let

{\mathscr{J}}^{(1)}\colon{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})\to[0,+\infty]
be the functional
given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{J}}^{(1)}(\mu)}\>:=\>\begin{cases}{\mathbb{I}}^{(1)}(\omega),&\text{if }\mu(\cdot)=\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}}\omega({\mathcal{M}})\,\delta_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\cdot)\text{ for }\omega\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{1})\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(2.14)

Proposition 2.4
.

Assume that condition H is in force.
Then,
\theta_{\epsilon}^{(1)}\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}

\Gamma
-converges to

{\mathscr{J}}^{(1)}
.

If the Markov chain
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{1}
has only one closed
irreducible class, then the metastable description of

X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
ended. Otherwise,
X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
exhibits a
metastable behavior in a longer timescale.

Higher order metastable structure.
In this
subsection, we describe the metastable behavior of the process

Y_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
across all time scales. The exposition is based on
a recursive construction proposed in
[
27
]
.

The recursive construction produces an integer
{\mathfrak{q}}\geq 1
, and triplets

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Gamma_{q}=({\mathtt{P}}_{q},\;{\mathfrak{h}}_{q},\;{\mathbb{L}}_{q})},\quad 1\leq q\leq{\mathfrak{q}}\,,

where
{\mathtt{P}}_{q}:={\mathscr{S}}_{q}\cup\{{\mathscr{T}}_{q}\}
is a
partition of the set
{\mathscr{M}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{S}}_{q}}:=\{{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j):j\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
is a family
of disjoint, non-empty subsets of
{\mathscr{M}}
,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{q}>0
is an energy
barrier, and
{\mathbb{L}}_{q}
is the generator of a

{\mathscr{S}}_{q}
-valued Markov chain, denoted by
{\mathbb{X}}_{q}(\cdot)
.
The first triplet
\Gamma_{1}
has been constructed in the
previous subsection.

It is shown in
[
27
]
that each triplet
\Gamma_{q}
,

1\leq q\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
, satisfies postulates
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(q)
–
{\mathcal{P}}_{10}(q)

listed in Appendix
A
of this article. We present below the
properties of this constructions needed to state the main theorem.
The reader can check that they are fulfilled by the triplet

\Gamma_{1}
. Fix
2\leq q\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
.

(a)

The sets
{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j)
are well ordered in the sense that
x<y

for all
x\in{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j)
,
y\in{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j+1)
,
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. [
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(q)
].

(b)

The potential
S(\cdot)
restricted to the sets
{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j)
is
constant. For each
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
S(y)=S(x)
for all
y
,

x\in{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j)
. The common value is denoted by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j))}
. [
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}(q)]
.

(c)

The sets
{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j)
are periodic in the sense that there
exists and integer
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{u}}_{q}}
such that

{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j+{\mathfrak{u}}_{q})={\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j)+1
. By the previous construction

{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}=N
. [
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}(q)
].

(d)

By construction, each set
{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j)
,
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, is
obtained as the union of a
{\mathbb{X}}_{q-1}
-closed irreducible
class. That is, for each
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, there exists a finite subset

A_{q,j}
of
{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
\{{\mathcal{M}}_{q-1}(i):i\in A_{q,j}\}

is a
{\mathbb{X}}_{q-1}
-closed irreducible class, and

{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j)=\cup_{i\in A_{q,j}}{\mathscr{M}}_{q-1}(i)
.

We may now define the escape barrier
{\mathfrak{h}}_{q}
. Denote by

m^{+}_{q,i}
,
m^{-}_{q,i}
,
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, the rightmost, leftmost
element of
{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(i)
, respectively:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m^{-}_{q,i}}\,:=\,\min\{m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(i)\}\;,\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m^{+}_{q,i}}\,:=\,\max\{m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(i)\}.

(2.15)

Let
{\mathscr{W}}^{(q)}_{k,k+1}
be the set of the absolute maximum of
S
on
the intervals
[m^{+}_{q,k},m^{-}_{q,k+1}]
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{W}}^{(q)}_{k,k+1}}\;:=\;\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{x\;\in\;[m^{+}_{q,k}\,,\,m^{-}_{q,k+1}]}S(x)\,,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{W}}_{q}}\,:=\,\bigcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathscr{W}}^{(q)}_{k,k+1}\;.

(2.16)

Clearly, the potential
S(\cdot)
is constant on

{\mathscr{W}}^{(q)}_{k,k+1}
. Denote by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S({\mathscr{W}}^{(q)}_{k,k+1})}
the
common value. Let
\sigma_{k,k+1}^{p,+}
,
\sigma_{k,k+1}^{p,-}
be
the rightmost and leftmost maxima of
{\mathscr{W}}^{(p)}_{k,k+1}
,
respectively:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma^{p,+}_{k,k+1}}\,:=\,\max\{\sigma\in{\mathscr{W}}^{(p)}_{k,k+1}\}\,,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}}\,:=\,\min\{\sigma\in{\mathscr{W}}^{(p)}_{k-1,k}\}\,,\quad k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\,.

(2.17)

Since the sets
{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k)
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, are ordered and the
elements of each set have the same depth, we may define the heights of
the left and right escape barriers of the set
{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k)
,

k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, as

\begin{gathered}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}h^{q,+}_{k}}\,:=\,S({\mathscr{W}}^{(q)}_{k,k+1})\,-\,S({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k))\;,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}h^{q,-}_{k}}\,:=\,S({\mathscr{W}}^{(q)}_{k-1,k})\,-\,S({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k))\;.\end{gathered}

(2.18)

Define the height
h^{q}_{k}
of the escape barrier of

{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k)
as the minimum of its left and right heights. Let

{\mathfrak{h}}_{q}
be the smallest height and

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(q)}
the associated time-scale:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}h^{q}_{k}}:=h^{q,-}_{k}\wedge h^{q,+}_{k}\;,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{h}}_{q}}:=\min\big\{\,h^{q}_{k}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\,\big\}\,,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\theta_{\epsilon}^{(q)}}\,:=\,e^{{\mathfrak{h}}_{q}/\epsilon}\,.

(2.19)

By property (c) of the sets
{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j)
,

h^{q}_{k+{\mathfrak{u}}_{q}}\,=\,h^{q}_{k}
for all
k\,\in\,{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Hence, the heights
h^{q}_{k}
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, take at most
{\mathfrak{u}}_{q}

different values, and
{\mathfrak{h}}^{q}\in(0,\infty)
. Moreover, by

[
27
, Proposition 2.3]
,

{\mathfrak{h}}_{q}\,>\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{q-1}\,.

(2.20)

Let the weight
\pi_{q}(k)
of the set of local minima

{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k)
and the weight
\sigma_{q}(k,k+1)
of the set of
maxima
{\mathscr{W}}^{(q)}_{k,k+1}
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, be defined as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{q}(k)}\,:=\,\pi_{1}({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k))\,=\,\sum_{m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k)}\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(m)}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{S^{\prime\prime}(m)}}\,,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{q}(k,k+1)}\,:=\,\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathscr{W}}^{(q)}_{k,k+1}}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{-\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma)}}\;\cdot

(2.21)

Define the jump rates as

\begin{gathered}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}R_{q}({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k+1))}\;:=\;\frac{1}{\pi_{q}(k)\,\sigma_{q}(k,k+1)}\;{\mathbf{1}}\{\,h^{q,+}_{k}\,=\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{q}\,\}\;,\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}R_{q}({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k-1))}\;:=\;\frac{1}{\pi_{q}(k)\,\sigma_{q}(k-1,k)}\;{\mathbf{1}}\{\,h^{q,-}_{k}\,=\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{q}\,\}\;.\end{gathered}

(2.22)

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{X}}_{q}(\cdot)}
be the
{\mathscr{S}}_{q}-
valued
Markov chain induced by the generator
{\mathbb{L}}_{q}
given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({\mathbb{L}}_{q}f)\,({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k))}\;=\;\sum_{a=\pm 1}R_{q}({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k\pm a))\,[\,f({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k+a))-f({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k))\,]\;,

(2.23)

for any
f\colon{\mathscr{S}}_{q}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and

{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k)\in{\mathscr{S}}_{q}
.

Observe that the Markov chain
{\mathbb{X}}_{q}
only jumps to
“nearest-neighbor” sets:
R_{q}({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j),{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k))=0
if

|k-j|\neq 1
. This remark together with property (d) explain property
(a) of the sets
{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j)
.

Remark 2.5
.

This remarks also holds for
q=1
. Since

\pi_{q}(k)\,\sigma_{q}(k,k\pm 1)
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, is strictly
positive and finite, by definition of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{q}
, there exists

k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
a\in\{-1,1\}
such that

R_{q}({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k^{\prime}),{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k^{\prime}+a))>0
. Therefore, the state

{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k^{\prime})
is either a transient state or it belongs to a

{\mathbb{X}}_{q}
-closed irreducible class with at least two elements.

Remark 2.6
.

By conditions (c), the variable
{\mathfrak{u}}_{q}
,
1\leq q\leq{\mathfrak{q}}

represents the number of states of the Markov chain
{\mathbb{X}}_{q}
up to
equivalence. By conditions (d), for
2\leq q\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
,
{\mathfrak{u}}_{q}
is
also the number of
{\mathbb{X}}_{q-1}
-closed irreducible classes, up to
equivalence. By Remark
2.5
, the sequence
{\mathfrak{u}}_{p}
strictly
decreases. The recursive construction ends when the Markov chain

{\mathbb{X}}_{p}(\cdot)
has a unique closed irreducible class, the whole set

{\mathscr{S}}_{p}
, or none (if all states are transient).

As
{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
,
{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)
are periodic functions, by
property (c) of the sets
{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j)
, the jump rates

R_{q}(\cdot,\,\cdot)
are periodic as well:

R_{q}({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k+{\mathfrak{u}}_{q}),{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k+{\mathfrak{u}}_{q}\pm 1))=R_{q}({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k\pm 1))
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Therefore,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{q}(\cdot)}:=\Pi({\mathbb{X}}_{q}(\cdot))
is a
continuous-time Markov chain taking values in

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{S}}_{q}}:=\{{\mathcal{M}}_{q}(k);0\leq k<{\mathfrak{u}}_{q}\}
, where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{M}}_{q}(k)}=\Pi({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k))
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}}
be the first integer
p\geq 1
such that

\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}
has a unique closed irreducible class. Clearly,

\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
, and it may happen that

\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}={\mathfrak{q}}-1
. See Figure
6
in Appendix

A
. Indeed, suppose that the Markov chain
{\mathbb{X}}_{p}
has
infinitely many closed irreducible classes, which are all
equivalent. In this case, we may construct a new layer by the
recursive procedure. In this new layer, the states will be the closed
irreducible classes of level
p
. As they are all equivalent and the
jump rates periodic, either all states are transient or there is only
one closed irreducible class. In particular, the recursive procedure
ends at this stage and
{\mathfrak{q}}=p+1
. On the other hand, the projected
Markov chain
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}
has only one closed irreducible
class so that
\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}=p
. We describe in Lemma
9.1
all
possible behaviors of the Markov chain
{\mathbb{X}}_{\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}}
and
the relation between
\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
and
{\mathfrak{q}}
in each case.

Denote by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{q}}
,
1\leq q\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
, the
generator of the projected Markov chain
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{q}(\cdot)
,
and let
{\mathbb{I}}^{(q)}\colon{\textsf{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{q})\to[0,+\infty)
be the
associated large deviations rate functional:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{I}}^{(q)}(\mu)}\,:=\,\sup_{H}-\,\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{q}}\frac{(\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{q}H)({\mathcal{M}})}{H({\mathcal{M}})}\mu({\mathcal{M}})\,,

(2.24)

where the supremum is performed over all positive functions

H\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{R}}
.

The definition of the rate functional
{\mathscr{J}}^{(q)}
requires some
further notation. Extend the definitions of he weights
\pi_{1}(\cdot)
,
introduced in (
2.11
), to the torus by setting

\pi_{1}(\{{\mathfrak{m}}\})=\pi_{p}(\Pi^{-1}(\{{\mathfrak{m}}\}))
. This is well defined
because
{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
and
{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)
are periodic. Let

\mu^{p}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
,
1\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
,

1\leq k\leq{\mathfrak{u}}_{p}
, be the measures defined by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mu^{p}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)}(\cdot)}\,:=\,\frac{1}{\pi_{p}(k)}\sum_{{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)}\pi_{1}(\{{\mathfrak{m}}\})\,\delta_{{\mathfrak{m}}}(\cdot)\,,

(2.25)

where
\pi_{p}(k)
has been introduced in (
2.21
), and turns

\mu^{p}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)}
into a probability measure. Informally,

\mu_{{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)}^{p}(\{m\})
,
m\in{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)
, represents the
proportion of time the diffusion
X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
spends close to
the point
m
when it visits the set
{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)
.

For
2\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
, let

{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}\colon{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})\to[0,+\infty]
be the functional
given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}(\mu)}\>:=\>\begin{cases}{\mathbb{I}}^{(p)}(\omega),&\text{if }\mu(\cdot)=\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\omega({\mathcal{M}})\,\mu^{p}_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\cdot)\text{ for }\omega\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{p})\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(2.26)

This functional can be regarded as the lifted version of
{\mathbb{I}}^{(p)}

to
{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
. We are now in position to state the main result of
this article.

Theorem 2.7
.

Assume that condition H is in force. Then, for
2\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
,

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}

\Gamma
-converges to

{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}
. Moreover, all conditions of Definition

2.1
are fulfilled and we can write

{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}\;=\;\frac{1}{\epsilon}\;{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}\;+\;{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}\;+\;\sum_{p=1}^{\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}}\frac{1}{\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}}\,{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}\;.

Organization of the paper

The rest of the article is organized as follows. In Section
3
,
we derive an explicit formula for the rate functional

{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
and prove Proposition
2.2
, which constitutes the first
step of the expansion. Section
4
is devoted to the proof of Proposition

2.3
. In Section
5
, we introduce a representation
formula for the rate-function of the reduced Markov chain

\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}(\cdot)
,
1\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
, originally derived
in
[
20
]
, and state the resolvent condition to
metastability. These results are the main technical and theoretical
tools required to derive the higher order expansion. In Section

6
, we verify the third condition in Definition

2.1
and establish the
\Gamma-\liminf
at every
metastable scale. The
\Gamma-\limsup
at metastable regimes is
discussed along the remaining three sections. In Section
7
,
we introduce a method to prove the
\Gamma-\limsup
at every
metastable scale with the exception of the last one. In Section

8
we extend this method to the last time-scale, under the
additional assumption that the Markov chain
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}}(\cdot)
is either reducible or reversible. Since this argument
breaks down in the complementary case, Section
9
is devoted
to the remaining situation in which
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}}(\cdot)
is
irreducible and non-reversible. There we establish the

\Gamma-\limsup
at the last metastable time-scale and complete the
proofs of Proposition
2.4
and Theorem
2.7
.

3.
Proof of Proposition
2.2

In this section, we establish several properties of the action
functional
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
and prove Proposition
2.2
. The
section is organized as follows. In Lemmata
3.1
and
3.2
,
we provide an explicit formula for
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu)
when the
measure
\mu
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue
measure and has a smooth, strictly positive density. Using this
explicit formula, in Corollary
3.3
we construct a recovery
sequence and prove the
\Gamma-\limsup
for absolutely continuous
measures. This recovery sequence is obtained by smoothing the density
and shifting it away from the origin.

To extend the
\Gamma-\limsup
to singular measures, we first construct
in Lemma
3.4
a recovery sequence for a Dirac measure supported
at a single point. Then, in Corollary
3.5
, using the convexity
of the rate functional
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
and the linearity of

{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}(\cdot)
when restricted to singular measures, we obtain
a recovery sequence and establish the
\Gamma-\limsup
for singular
measures. By decomposing a general measure into its absolutely
continuous and singular parts, we can extend the
\Gamma-\limsup
proof
to all measures, again relying on the convexity of

{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
. This final step is carried out in the second
part of the proof of Proposition
2.2
, towards the end of this
section.

The
\Gamma-\liminf
part of the proof is obtained in two steps. First,
exploiting the variational representation of the rate functional

{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
and choosing test functions of the form

u_{\epsilon}=e^{F/\epsilon}
, for a smooth function
F(\cdot)
, we
derive a lower bound. Optimizing over
F
completes the argument.
Since
{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}(\mu)
is finite for all measures

\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
, it follows that
{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}(\mu)
appears as
the first term in the
\Gamma
-expansion of the rate functional

{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
. This is the content of Lemma

3.6
, and it completes the proof of Proposition

2.2
.

3.1.
Properties of the rate functional
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}

For a function

H\in C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
, denote by
{\mathscr{L}}_{H,\epsilon}
the generator given
by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({\mathscr{L}}_{H,\epsilon}F)(x)}\;=\;\{\,{\textsf{b}}\,+\,2\,\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}\,H^{\prime}\,\}F^{\prime}\;+\;\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}\,F^{\prime\prime}\;.

Thus, the diffusion induced by the generator
{\mathscr{L}}_{H,\epsilon}
has
an extra drift
2\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}\,H^{\prime}
with respect to the one induced by

{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
m\in C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
be the density of a probability measure
(
m\geq 0
,
\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}m(x)\,dx=1
). Suppose that there exists

c>0
such that
m(x)\geq c
for all
x\in{\mathbb{T}}
. Let

H\colon{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be given by

H(x)\;=\;\frac{1}{2}\,\log\big({\textsf{a}}(x)\,m(x)\big)\;+\;\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\frac{\langle{\textsf{b}}/{\textsf{a}}\rangle}{\langle 1/({\textsf{a}}\,m)\rangle}\,\int_{0}^{x}\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(y)\,m(y)}\,dy\,-\,\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\int_{0}^{x}\frac{{\textsf{b}}(y)}{{\textsf{a}}(y)}\,dy\;,

(3.1)

where, recall,
\langle h\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}h(y)\,dy
. Then,
H
belongs to

C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
and
m
is the unique stationary state for the diffusion
induced by the generator
{\mathscr{L}}_{H,\epsilon}
.

Proof.

An elementary computation yields that
H
belongs to
C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
and
that

\epsilon\,\big({\textsf{a}}\,m\big)^{\prime\prime}\,-\,\big({\textsf{b}}\,m\big)^{\prime}\,-\,2\epsilon\,\big({\textsf{a}}\,H^{\prime}\,m\big)^{\prime}\;=\;0\;.

Hence, by integration by part,

\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}({\mathscr{L}}_{H,\epsilon}F)(x)\,m(x)\,dx\;=\;0\,,

for any function
F\in C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
, as claimed.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
be such that
\mu(dx)=m(x)\,dx
, where

m\in C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
and there exists
c>0
such that
m(x)\geq c
for
all
x\in{\mathbb{T}}
. Then,

{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu)\;=\;-\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}e^{H}}{e^{H}}\;d\mu\;=\;\epsilon\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}\,[H^{\prime}]^{2}\;d\mu\;=\;\frac{1}{4\,\epsilon}\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}\,\Big\{\,\epsilon\,\Big[\frac{m^{\prime}}{m}+\frac{{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}}{{\textsf{a}}}\Big]\,+\,\frac{B\,/\,({\textsf{a}}\,m)}{\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}1\,/({\textsf{a}}\,m)\,dy}\,-\,\frac{{\textsf{b}}}{{\textsf{a}}}\,\Big\}^{2}\,d\mu\;,

where
H
is given by (
3.1
).

Proof.

Fix
G\in C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
. An elementary computation yields that

\frac{{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}e^{G}}{e^{G}}\;=\;{\textsf{b}}\,G^{\prime}\,+\,\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}\,(G^{\prime})^{2}\,+\;\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}\,G^{\prime\prime}\,.

(3.2)

Adding and subtracting

2\,\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}\,G^{\prime}\,H^{\prime}
, yields that

\frac{{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}e^{G}}{e^{G}}\;=\;{\mathscr{L}}_{H,\epsilon}G\;+\;\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}\,[G^{\prime}]^{2}\;-\;2\,\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}\,G^{\prime}\,H^{\prime}\;.

(3.3)

Completing the square, and since, by Lemma
3.1
,
\mu
is the
stationary state of the diffusion induced by the generator
{\mathscr{L}}_{H,\epsilon}
,

-\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}e^{G}}{e^{G}}\;d\mu\;\leq\;-\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\mathscr{L}}_{H,\epsilon}G\;d\mu\;+\;\epsilon\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}\,[H^{\prime}]^{2}\;d\mu\;=\;\epsilon\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}\,[H^{\prime}]^{2}\;d\mu\;.

Taking
G=H
in equation (
3.3
) yields that

-\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}e^{H}}{e^{H}}\;d\mu\;=\;\epsilon\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}\,[H^{\prime}]^{2}\;d\mu\;,

which completes the proof of the lemma in view of the penultimate
displayed equation inequality.
∎

Corollary 3.3
.

Let
\mu(dx)=m(x)\,dx\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
be a measure absolutely
continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure. Then, there exists a
sequence
\mu_{\epsilon}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
, such that
\mu_{\epsilon}

converges weakly to
\mu
as
\epsilon\to 0
, and

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\;\leq\;\frac{1}{4}\,\Big\{\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{{\textsf{b}}^{2}}{{\textsf{a}}}\,d\mu\;-\;\frac{1}{\langle 1/({\textsf{a}}\,m)\rangle}\,B^{2}\,\Big\}\;.

Proof.

If
\mu
satisfies the assumptions of the previous lemma, consider the
constant sequence
\mu_{\epsilon}=\mu
. The assertion of the corollary
is a straightforward consequence of the formula for

{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu)
presented in Lemma
3.2
.

Fix a measure
\mu(dx)=m(x)\,dx\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
such that

m(x)\geq c
for all
x\in{\mathbb{T}}
and some
c>0
. In this case,
smoothing the density
m
we obtain a sequence
m_{n}
satisfying the
hypotheses of Lemma
3.2
and such that
\mu_{n}(dx)=m_{n}(x)\,dx

converges weakly to
\mu(dx)
. Clearly,

{\mathscr{J}}(\mu_{n})\to{\mathscr{J}}(\mu)
. A diagonal argument permits to
construct a sequence
\mu_{\epsilon}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
such that

\mu_{\epsilon}\to\mu
weakly as
\epsilon\to 0
, and

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\;\leq{\mathscr{J}}(\mu)
.

Finally, fix a measure
\mu(dx)=m(x)\,dx\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
. Let

m_{\epsilon}(x)=(m(x)+\epsilon)/(1+\epsilon)
. Then,

\mu_{\epsilon}(dx)=m_{\epsilon}(x)\,dx
converges weakly to

\mu(dx)
as
\epsilon\to 0
and

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}{\mathscr{J}}(\mu_{\epsilon})={\mathscr{J}}(\mu)
.
The same diagonal argument permits to conclude the proof.
∎

3.2.
Recovery sequence for singular measures

Fix
x\in{\mathbb{T}}
, define the interval

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}I_{x}}:=(x-1/4,\,x+1/4)
, and consider a function

G\in C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
such that

\displaystyle G(y)\,=\,\frac{\|y-x\|^{2}}{2}\,,\,\,y\in I_{x}\,,\quad\quad G(y)\,\geq\,\frac{1}{32}\,,\,\,y\in{\mathbb{T}}\setminus I_{x}\,.

In particular,
G(y)=0
if, and only if,
y=x
,
and
G^{\prime}(x)=0
. Let

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mu_{\epsilon}(dy)}\,:=\;\frac{e^{-G(y)/\epsilon}}{\langle e^{-G/\epsilon}\rangle}\;dy\,.

(3.4)

Lemma 3.4
.

Fix
x\in{\mathbb{T}}
, and consider the sequence of measures

\mu_{\epsilon}
given by (
3.4
). Then, as
\epsilon\to 0
,

\mu_{\epsilon}
converges weakly to
\delta_{x}
, and

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\;=\;\frac{1}{4}\,\frac{{\textsf{b}}(x)^{2}}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,.

Proof.

For every
\phi\in C({\mathbb{T}})
and
\delta\in(0,1)
,

\left|\langle\mu_{\epsilon},\phi\rangle-\langle\delta_{x},\phi\rangle\right|\;\leq\;\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in{\mathbb{T}}\\
\|y-x\|<\delta\end{subarray}}\left|\phi(y)-\phi(x)\right|\;+\;2\,\|\phi\|_{\infty}\,\frac{1}{\langle e^{-G/\epsilon}\rangle}\,\int_{\|y-x\|>\delta}e^{-G(y)/\epsilon}\,dy\;,

which, by the continuity of
\phi
at
x\in{\mathbb{T}}
and the quadratic
behavior of
G
at
x
, vanishes taking first
\epsilon\to 0
, and
then
\delta\to 0
. This proves that
\mu_{\epsilon}
converges to

\delta_{x}
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

On the other hand, by Lemma
3.2
,

\epsilon\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\;=\;\frac{1}{4}\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}\,\Big\{\,\epsilon\,\Big[\frac{m^{\prime}_{\epsilon}}{m_{\epsilon}}\,+\,\frac{{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}}{{\textsf{a}}}\Big]\,+\,\frac{B\,/\,({\textsf{a}}\,m_{\epsilon})}{\langle 1/({\textsf{a}}\,m_{\epsilon})\rangle}\,-\,\frac{{\textsf{b}}}{{\textsf{a}}}\,\Big\}^{2}\,d\mu_{\epsilon}\;,

where
m_{\epsilon}\,=\,e^{-G/\epsilon}/\langle e^{-G/\epsilon}\rangle
. Expand
the square. It is not difficult to show that all terms vanish as

\epsilon\to 0
, except

(1/4)\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{b}}^{2}\,/\,{\textsf{a}}\,d\mu_{\epsilon}
which
converges to
{\textsf{b}}(x)^{2}\,/4\,{\textsf{a}}(x)
since
\mu_{\epsilon}

weakly converges to
\delta_{x}
.
∎

Corollary 3.5
.

Fix
\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
. Then, there exists a
sequence
\mu_{\epsilon}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
, such that
\mu_{\epsilon}

converges weakly to
\mu
as
\epsilon\to 0
, and

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\;\leq\;\frac{1}{4}\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{{\textsf{b}}^{2}}{{\textsf{a}}}\,d\mu\;,

Proof.

Fix
\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
, and let
{\mathscr{J}}_{0}\colon{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be given by

{\mathscr{J}}_{0}(\mu)=(1/4)\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{b}}^{2}\,/\,{\textsf{a}}\,d\mu
.

By Lemma
3.4
, the assertion of the corollary holds for Dirac
measures. Consider a sequence
\mu_{n}
of finite convex combinations
of Dirac measures which converges weakly to
\mu
. Since
b
and

a
are continuous,
{\mathscr{J}}_{0}(\mu_{n})\to{\mathscr{J}}_{0}(\mu)
. On the
other hand, by the convexity of
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
, the linearity of

{\mathscr{J}}_{0}
and Lemma
3.4
,

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{n})\;\leq\;{\mathscr{J}}_{0}(\mu_{n})

for all
n\geq 1
. A diagonal argument provides a sequence which
satisfies the assertions of the corollary.
∎

Observe that the previous corollary provides a recovery sequence and
the
\Gamma-\limsup
for singular measures.

\Gamma
-convergence of
\epsilon\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}

In this subsection, we prove the
\Gamma
-convergence of the action
functional
\epsilon\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
, as stated in Proposition

2.2
.

Proof of Proposition
2.2
.

We prove the
\Gamma-\liminf
and
\Gamma-\limsup
inequalities
separately.

\Gamma-\liminf
. Fix

\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
, and let
\mu_{\epsilon}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
be a
sequence such that
\mu_{\epsilon}\to\mu
. Let

u_{\epsilon}:=e^{F/\epsilon}
for some
F\in C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
. By
(
3.2
), by the definition of
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
and since
F
belongs to

C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
,

\epsilon\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\;\geq\;\epsilon\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}-\frac{{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}}{u_{\epsilon}}\;d\mu_{\epsilon}\;=\;-\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\big(\,{\textsf{b}}+{\textsf{a}}\,F^{\prime}\,\big)\,F^{\prime}\;d\mu_{\epsilon}\;+\;O(\epsilon)\;.

Therefore, as
\mu_{\epsilon}\to\mu
, and
{\textsf{a}},{\textsf{b}}
and
F^{\prime}
are
continuous functions,

\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\;\geq\;-\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\big(\,{\textsf{b}}+{\textsf{a}}\,F^{\prime}\,\big)\,F^{\prime}\;d\mu\;.

Fix a function
h
in
L^{2}(\mu)\cap L^{1}(dx)
, such that
h\geq 0
,

\langle h/{\textsf{a}}\rangle>0
, and let

H=(1/2)\,\big(\frac{{\textsf{b}}}{{\textsf{a}}}-\frac{B\,h/{\textsf{a}}}{\langle h\,/\,{\textsf{a}}\rangle}\,\big)
. Note that
\langle H\rangle=0
. Consider a sequence

F_{n}
of
C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
functions such that
F^{\prime}_{n}
converges to
-H
in

L^{2}(\mu)
to conclude that

\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\;\geq\;\frac{1}{4}\,\Big\{\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{{\textsf{b}}^{2}}{{\textsf{a}}}\;d\mu\;-\;\frac{\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}h^{2}/{\textsf{a}}\;d\mu}{\langle h/{\textsf{a}}\rangle^{2}}\,B^{2}\,\Big\}\;\cdot

(3.5)

To complete the proof, it remains to optimize in
h
. This is done in
the next paragraph.

Recall that we denote by
\mu_{\rm ac}
,
\mu_{\rm s}
the absolutely
continuous and the singular part, respectively, of the measure
\mu

with respect to the Lebesgue measure
\lambda
, and by
m_{\mu}
the
Radon-Nikodym derivative of
\mu_{\rm ac}
with respect to
\lambda
.

Since
\mu_{\rm s}
is the singular part, there exists a measurable
set
A
such that
\mu_{\rm s}(A)=0
and
\lambda(A)=1
. Let

h_{\epsilon}=\chi_{A}\,(1+\epsilon)/(m_{\mu}+\epsilon)
. It is easy
to see that the function
h_{\epsilon}
satisfies the hypothesis needed
to derive (
3.5
). By definition of
A
,

\frac{\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}h_{\epsilon}^{2}/{\textsf{a}}\;d\mu}{\langle h_{\epsilon}/{\textsf{a}}\rangle^{2}}\;=\;\frac{\langle\,m_{\mu}/(\,{\textsf{a}}\,[m_{\mu}+\epsilon]^{2}\,)\,\rangle}{\langle\,1/(\,{\textsf{a}}\,[m_{\mu}+\epsilon]\,)\,\rangle^{2}}\;\cdot

If
m_{\mu}^{-1}
is Lebesgue integrable, by the monotone convergence
theorem, this expression converges to

1\,/\,\langle 1/\,{\textsf{a}}\,m_{\mu}\rangle
. If it is not, observe that it is
bounded by

\frac{\langle 1/(\,{\textsf{a}}\,[m_{\mu}+\epsilon]\,)\,\rangle}{\langle\,1/(\,{\textsf{a}}\,[m_{\mu}+\epsilon]\,)\,\rangle^{2}}\;=\;\frac{1}{\langle 1/(\,{\textsf{a}}\,[m_{\mu}+\epsilon]\,)\,\rangle}\;\to\;0\;=\;\frac{1}{\langle 1/{\textsf{a}}\,m_{\mu}\rangle}\;\cdot

Thus, in both cases, the expression on the right-hand side of the
penultimate formula converges to
1/\langle 1/{\textsf{a}}m_{\mu}\rangle
as

\epsilon\to 0
. This shows that

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\epsilon\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\;\geq\;\frac{1}{4}\,\Big\{\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{{\textsf{b}}^{2}}{{\textsf{a}}}\;d\mu\;-\;\frac{1}{\langle 1/{\textsf{a}}m_{\mu}\rangle}\,B^{2}\,\Big\}\;,

and completes the proof of the
\Gamma-\liminf
.

\Gamma-\limsup
. Fix

\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
, and recall that we denote by
\mu_{\rm ac}
,

\mu_{\rm s}
the absolutely continuous and the singular part,
respectively, of the measure
\mu
with respect to the Lebesgue
measure. Let
r=\mu_{\rm ac}({\mathbb{T}})
to rewrite
\mu
as

\mu=r\mu^{*}_{\rm ac}+(1-r)\mu^{*}_{\rm s}
, where

\mu^{*}_{\rm ac}=(1/r)\mu_{\rm ac}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
,

\mu^{*}_{\rm s}=[1/(1-r)]\mu_{\rm s}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
.
We assume here that
0<r<1
, but the argument is simpler if
r(1-r)=0
.

Denote by
\mu_{\epsilon}
the measure given by Corollary
3.3

associated to
\mu^{*}_{\rm ac}
, and by
\nu_{\epsilon}
the one given by
Corollary
3.5
associated to
\mu^{*}_{\rm s}
. By these results,
as
\epsilon\to 0
,

\varrho_{\epsilon}=r\mu_{\epsilon}+(1-r)\nu_{\epsilon}
converges
weakly to
r\mu^{*}_{\rm ac}+(1-r)\mu^{*}_{\rm s}=\mu
. On the other
hand, by convexity of
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
,

{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\varrho_{\epsilon})\leq r\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})+(1-r)\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\nu_{\epsilon})
. Hence, by
Corollaries
3.3
and
3.5
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\epsilon\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\varrho_{\epsilon})\;\leq\;\frac{r}{4}\,\Big\{\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{{\textsf{b}}^{2}}{{\textsf{a}}}\;d\mu^{*}_{\rm ac}\;-\;\frac{1}{\langle 1/{\textsf{a}}m_{\mu^{*}_{\rm ac}}\rangle}\,B^{2}\,\Big\}\;+\;\frac{1-r}{4}\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{{\textsf{b}}^{2}}{{\textsf{a}}}\;d\mu^{*}_{\rm s}

\displaystyle=\;\frac{1}{4}\,\Big\{\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{{\textsf{b}}^{2}}{{\textsf{a}}}\;d\mu\;-\;\frac{1}{\langle 1/r{\textsf{a}}m_{\mu^{*}_{\rm ac}}\rangle}\,B^{2}\,\Big\}\;=\;\frac{1}{4}\,\Big\{\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{{\textsf{b}}^{2}}{{\textsf{a}}}\;d\mu\;-\;\frac{1}{\langle 1/{\textsf{a}}m_{\mu}\rangle}\,B^{2}\,\Big\}\;.

This establishes the
\Gamma-\limsup
. Lemma
3.6

below completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

We conclude this section showing that
\epsilon
is the shortest
time-scale at which the rate functional
{\mathscr{J}}_{\epsilon}
exhibits a
nontrivial limit in the sense of
\Gamma-
convergence.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
(\beta_{\epsilon}:\epsilon>0)
be any positive sequence such that

\beta_{\epsilon}\prec\epsilon
. Then,
\beta_{\epsilon}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}

\Gamma-
converges to
0
.

Proof.

Fix a sequence
\beta_{\epsilon}
such that

\beta_{\epsilon}\prec\epsilon
. We prove the
\Gamma-\liminf
and

\Gamma-\limsup
inequalities separately.

\Gamma-\liminf
.
Fix
\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})

and let
\mu_{\epsilon}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
be a sequence such that

\mu_{\epsilon}
converges weakly to
\mu
. By (
2.4
),

{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}(\mu)<+\infty
. Then, by Proposition
2.2
and the
definition of
\Gamma-
convergence,

\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\beta_{\epsilon}\,{\mathscr{I}}(\mu_{\epsilon})\,\geq\,\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{\beta_{\epsilon}}{\epsilon}\,{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}(\mu)\,=\,0\,.

\Gamma-\limsup
.
Fix

\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
. By Proposition
2.2
, choose a sequence

\mu_{\epsilon}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
such that
\mu_{\epsilon}
converges
weakly to
\mu
and

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\,\leq\,{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}(\mu).

Then, since
\beta_{\epsilon}\prec\epsilon
,

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\beta_{\epsilon}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\leq\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{\beta_{\epsilon}}{\epsilon}{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}(\mu)=0,

given that
{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}(\mu)<+\infty
.
∎

4.
Proof of Proposition
2.3

In this section we establish Proposition
2.3
. The section is divided in two main parts. In the first one, we address the

\Gamma
–
\liminf
of the functional
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
. This is
Proposition
4.1
, whose proof is developed in three steps. In
Lemma
4.2
, we show that the zero-level set of the functional

{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}
consists of convex combinations of Dirac measures
supported on
{\mathcal{C}}
, the set of critical points. This observation
allows us to restrict our analysis to this convex hull. A key
ingredient in the proof of the
\Gamma-\liminf
is the introduction of
local versions of the potential
S
defined via cut-off
functions. However, this approach raises the technical challenge of
obtaining suitable estimates on the intervals containing the supports
of the derivatives of these cut-offs. Lemma
4.3
provides the
necessary bounds to overcome this difficulty. We conclude this part by
assembling all the intermediate results to complete the proof of the

\Gamma
–
\liminf
of
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
.

In the second part of the section, we turn to the
\Gamma
–
\limsup

of
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
, as stated in Proposition
4.4
. The
main step is Lemma
4.5
, which constructs a recovery
sequence for the
\Gamma
–
\limsup
corresponding to Dirac measures
supported on
{\mathcal{C}}
. By the convexity of
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
, this
suffices to prove Proposition
4.4
. As in the previous
subsection, we verify that the constructed sequence is indeed a
recovery sequence by applying the formula from Lemma
3.2
to
estimate
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
.

Second order
\Gamma-\liminf

The main result of this subsection reads as follows.

Proposition 4.1
.

Assume that condition
(H)
is in force. Then, the functional

{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}
introduced in (
2.5
) is a
\Gamma-\liminf
of
the sequence of rate functionals
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
.

The proof requires some preliminary estimates. We first show that the

0
-level set of the functional
{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}(\cdot)
is exactly the set of all convex
combinations of the Dirac measures
\delta_{x}
,
x\in{\mathcal{C}}
.

Lemma 4.2
.

A probability measure
\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
belongs to the
0
-level
set of the functional
{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}(\cdot)
if, and only if,

\mu(\cdot)=\sum_{z\in{\mathcal{C}}}\omega(z)\,\delta_{z}(\cdot)
for some

\omega\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathcal{C}})
.

Proof.

Assume that

\mu(\cdot)=\sum_{z\in{\mathcal{C}}}\omega(z)\,\delta_{z}(\cdot)
for some

\omega\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathcal{C}})
, and recall the notation introduced in
(
2.4
). Clearly
m_{\mu}=0
, and the second term on the right-hand
side of (
2.4
) vanishes. The first one also vanishes because
{\textsf{b}}(x)=0
for all
x\in{\mathcal{C}}
.

Conversely, suppose that
{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}(\mu)=0
for some

\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
. We have to show that
\mu({\mathcal{C}}^{c})=0
.

By Schwarz inequality,

\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{{\textsf{b}}^{2}}{{\textsf{a}}}\;d\mu_{\rm ac}\;-\;\frac{1}{\langle 1/({\textsf{a}}m_{\mu})\rangle}\,B^{2}\,\geq\,0\;.

(4.1)

Hence, if
{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}(\mu)=0
, the previous difference vanishes and

\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{{\textsf{b}}^{2}}{{\textsf{a}}}\;d\mu_{\rm s}\,=\,0\,.

Since
{\textsf{b}}(x)^{2}/{\textsf{a}}(x)>0
for
x\not\in{\mathcal{C}}
,

\mu_{\rm s}({\mathcal{C}}^{c})=0
.

We turn to the absolutely continuous part
\mu_{\rm ac}
. If

\langle 1/m_{\mu}\rangle=+\infty
, since the difference (
4.1
) vanishes, the
first integral vanishes. Therefore, as
{\textsf{b}}(x)^{2}/{\textsf{a}}(x)>0
for

x\not\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
\mu_{\rm ac}({\mathcal{C}}^{c})=0
, which completes the
proof of the lemma in the case
\langle 1/m_{\mu}\rangle=+\infty
.

It remains to consider the case
\langle 1/m_{\mu}\rangle<+\infty
. As the
difference (
4.1
) vanishes (and Schwarz inequality becomes an
identity), there exists
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

{\textsf{b}}\sqrt{m_{\mu}}/\sqrt{{\textsf{a}}}=c\,1/\sqrt{{\textsf{a}}\,m_{\mu}}

Lebesgue a.s. The constant
c
can not be
0
because we assumed that

\langle 1/m_{\mu}\rangle<+\infty
. If
c\neq 0
, then, as
m_{\mu}>0
Lebesgue
a.s.,
{\textsf{b}}=c/m_{\mu}
a.s., which is a contradiction because
b
changes sign while
m_{\mu}
is always positive. This completes the
proof of the lemma.
∎

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}B_{r}(x)}\subset{\mathbb{T}}
be the ball of radius
r>0
centered at

x\in{\mathbb{T}}
, and, for a finite subset
A
of
{\mathbb{T}}
, set

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}B_{r}(A)}:=\cup_{x\in A}B_{r}(x)
. Denote by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r_{0}}
the
minimal distance between points in
{\mathcal{C}}
. The proof of the

\Gamma-\liminf
relies on the following estimate.

Lemma 4.3
.

Fix
\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
, and a sequence

(\mu_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
,
\mu_{\epsilon}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
,
converging weakly to
\mu
. Suppose that

\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\,<\,\infty\,.

Then, for all
r<r_{0}/3
,
x\in{\mathcal{C}}
,

\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\mu_{\epsilon}({\mathbb{T}}\setminus B_{r}({\mathcal{C}})\,\big)\,<\,\infty\,.

Proof.

By the definition of the rate functional
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
and by
hypothesis, for any functions
F
in
C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})

\displaystyle\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}e^{F(x)/\epsilon}}{e^{F(x)/\epsilon}}\;d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\,\leq\,\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\,<\,\infty\,.

Thus, by (
3.2
), there exists a finite constant
C_{0}
such
that

\displaystyle\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\Big\{-\,\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\big[\,{\textsf{b}}(x)\,+\,{\textsf{a}}(x)\,F^{\prime}(x)\,\big]F^{\prime}(x)\;d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\;-\;\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}(x)\,F^{\prime\prime}(x)\;d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\,\Big\}\,\leq\,C_{0}\,.

As
F
belongs to
C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
, the second integral is bounded so that

\displaystyle\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\Big\{-\,\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}(x)\,\big[\,{\textsf{c}}(x)\,+\,F^{\prime}(x)\,\big]F^{\prime}(x)\;d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\,\Big\}\,\leq\,C_{0}\,,

(4.2)

for a new constant
C_{0}
whose value may change from line to line and
which may depend on the function
F
. Here

{\textsf{c}}(x)={\textsf{b}}(x)/{\textsf{a}}(x)
.

Fix
x\in{\mathbb{T}}
, and suppose that
{\textsf{c}}(x)\geq 0
. In this case,

[\,{\textsf{c}}(x)\,+\,F^{\prime}(x)\,\big]F^{\prime}(x)\leq 0
if, and only if,

F^{\prime}(x)\in[-{\textsf{c}}(x),0]
. If
{\textsf{c}}(x)\leq 0
, then

[\,{\textsf{c}}(x)\,+\,F^{\prime}(x)\,\big]F^{\prime}(x)\leq 0
if, and only if,
F^{\prime}(x)\in[0,-{\textsf{c}}(x)]
. Therefore, to fulfill the inequality,
F
has
to decrease in intervals where
{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)
is positive and to
increase in the intervals where
{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)
is negative. In
consequence, the test function
F(\cdot)
may not have support in an
interval formed by two consecutive critical points of
S(\cdot)
, and

F^{\prime}(\cdot)
has to vanish at these critical points.

In view of this observation, the construction of the test functions

F
is clear. Fix three consecutive critical points

x_{{\mathtt{l}}}<x_{0}<x_{{\mathtt{r}}}
, and
\delta>0
. Assume, without loss
of generality, that
{\textsf{c}}(x)\leq 0
for
x\in[x_{{\mathtt{l}}},x_{0}]
,
and, in consequence,
{\textsf{c}}(x)\geq 0
for
x\in[x_{0},x_{{\mathtt{r}}}]
. As
{\textsf{c}}(\cdot)
is continuous, there exists
\eta>0
such
that
{\textsf{c}}(x)\leq-\eta
for all

x\in[x_{{\mathtt{l}}}+\delta,x_{0}-\delta]
, and
{\textsf{c}}(x)\geq\eta
for
all
x\in[x_{0}+\delta,x_{{\mathtt{r}}}-\delta]
.

Fix a point
z_{{\mathtt{l}}}\in[x_{{\mathtt{l}}}+2\delta,x_{0}-5\delta]
and a point

z_{{\mathtt{r}}}\in[x_{0}+5\delta,x_{{\mathtt{r}}}-2\delta]
. Let

F\colon{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be given by

F(x)\,=\,(\eta/2)\,(x-z_{{\mathtt{l}}})\,\quad\text{for}\;\;x\in[z_{{\mathtt{l}}},z_{{\mathtt{l}}}+3\delta]\,.

In the interval
[z_{{\mathtt{l}}}+3\delta,z_{{\mathtt{r}}}-3\delta]
,
F
is
set to be constant, and

F(x)\,=\,(\eta/2)\,(z_{{\mathtt{r}}}-x)\,\quad\text{for}\;\;x\in[z_{{\mathtt{r}}}-3\delta,z_{{\mathtt{r}}}]\,.

Complete the definition of
F(\cdot)
by setting
F(y)=0
for

y\not\in[z_{{\mathtt{l}}},z_{{\mathtt{r}}}]
. In this way,

F(\cdot)
is a continuous function.

By definition of
F
, on the interval

[z_{{\mathtt{l}}},z_{{\mathtt{l}}}+3\delta]
, since
-\,{\textsf{c}}(\cdot)
is
bounded below by
\eta
on this interval,
{\textsf{c}}(x)\,+\,F^{\prime}(x)\leq-\,\eta/2
, so that

-\,{\textsf{a}}(x)\,\big[\,{\textsf{c}}(x)\,+\,F^{\prime}(x)\,\big]\,F^{\prime}(x)\,\geq\,(\eta/2)^{2}\,\inf_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}(x)\,.

(4.3)

A similar bound holds in the interval

[z_{{\mathtt{r}}}-3\delta,z_{{\mathtt{r}}}]
because
{\textsf{c}}(\cdot)
is
bounded below by
\eta
. On the complement of these intervals
F^{\prime}

vanishes.

If
F(\cdot)
were in the domain of the generator, we could directly
substitute it into equation (
4.2
) to complete the proof. However,
since
F^{\prime}(\cdot)
is discontinuous we must smooth
F(\cdot)
in a way
that preserves the previously established bounds in order to complete
the proof of the lemma.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Phi}\colon[-1,1]\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be a smooth approximation of
the identity: the support of
\Phi(\cdot)
is contained in the set

(-1,1)
, and
\int\Phi(x)\,dx=1
. Let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Phi_{a}(x)}:=(1/a)\,\Phi(x/a)
,
0<a<1
.

Let
F_{\delta}=F*\Phi_{\delta}
. The function
F_{\delta}
is smooth,
and thus belongs to the domain of the generator. We examine the
left-hand side of (
4.3
) with
F_{\delta}
in place of
F
. By
definition of
F(\cdot)
,

F^{\prime}_{\delta}(x)=\int F^{\prime}(x-y)\,\Phi_{\delta}(y)\,dy
vanishes in the
complement of

[z_{{\mathtt{l}}}-\delta,z_{{\mathtt{l}}}+4\delta]\cup[z_{{\mathtt{r}}}-4\delta,z_{{\mathtt{r}}}+\delta]
. On the intervals

[z_{{\mathtt{l}}}-\delta,z_{{\mathtt{l}}}+\delta]
,

[z_{{\mathtt{l}}}+2\delta,z_{{\mathtt{l}}}+4\delta]
, as

-\,{\textsf{c}}(x)\geq\eta
, and
0\leq F^{\prime}_{\delta}(x)\leq\eta/2
, the
left-hand side of (
4.3
) with
F_{\delta}
in place of
F
is
positive. On the interval

[z_{{\mathtt{l}}}+\delta,z_{{\mathtt{l}}}+2\delta]
,

F_{\delta}(x)=F^{\prime}(x)
so that (
4.3
) holds for
F_{\delta}
. A
similar analysis can be carried out on the interval

[z_{{\mathtt{r}}}-4\delta,z_{{\mathtt{r}}}+\delta]
. Therefore,

\displaystyle-\,\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}(x)\,\big[\,{\textsf{c}}(x)\,+\,F_{\delta}^{\prime}(x)\,\big]F_{\delta}^{\prime}(x)\;d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)

\displaystyle\quad\,\geq\,(1/2)\,\eta^{2}\,\inf_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}(x)\,\big\{\,\mu_{\epsilon}([z_{{\mathtt{l}}}+\delta,z_{{\mathtt{l}}}+2\delta])\,+\,\mu_{\epsilon}([z_{{\mathtt{r}}}-2\delta,z_{{\mathtt{r}}}-\delta])\,\big\}\,.

As the previous intervals have length
\delta
, choosing
z_{{\mathtt{l}}}
,

z_{{\mathtt{r}}}
appropriately yields that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\,\big\{\,\mu_{\epsilon}([\sigma_{j}+5\delta,m_{j}-5\delta])\,+\,\mu_{\epsilon}([m_{j}+5\delta,\sigma_{j+1}-5\delta])\,\big\}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\,\frac{-\,2}{\delta\,\eta^{2}}\,\frac{1}{\inf_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}(x)\,\big[\,{\textsf{c}}(x)\,+\,F_{\delta}^{\prime}(x)\,\big]F_{\delta}^{\prime}(x)\;d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\,.

This completes the proof of the lemma in view of (
4.2
).
∎

Given two real valued functions
f,g
, where
g
is positive, we say
that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f(x)=O(g(x))}
, if there exist some positive constant
C>0

such that
|f(x)|\leq Cg(x)
, for every
x
in the domain of
f
. If

f
is also positive, we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f(x)=\Theta(g(x))}
if
f
and

g
have the same domain, and both
f(x)=O(g(x))
and
g(x)=O(f(x))

hold. We are now in position to prove Proposition
4.1

\sigma
m
S(x_{\sigma,2}^{-})=S(x_{\sigma,2}^{+})
S(x_{\sigma,1}^{-})=S(x_{\sigma,1}^{+})
S(y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{2,-})=S(y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{2,+})
S(y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{1,-})=S(y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{1,+})
J_{\sigma}
J_{r,\epsilon}^{\sigma}
x_{\sigma,2}^{-}
x_{\sigma,1}^{-}
x_{\sigma,1}^{+}
x_{\sigma,2}^{+}
y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{2,+}
y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{1,+}
I_{\sigma}^{+}
I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}
I_{\sigma,\epsilon}
y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{2,-}
y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{1,-}
I_{\sigma}^{-}
I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}

Figure 1.
Here we illustrate the intervals constructed in the proof of
Proposition
4.1
. The intervals in orange satisfy

\mu_{\epsilon}(I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+})=O(\epsilon^{2})
. The union of these sets contain the support of the
derivative of the cut-off function
\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}
. More
precisely,
\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}
is strictly increasing on

I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}
, strictly decreasing on

I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}
, it is equal to
1
on
I_{\sigma,\epsilon}
,
and it vanishes on

(I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+})^{c}
.

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

Fix
\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
and

(\mu_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}\subset{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
such that

\mu_{\epsilon}
converges weakly to
\mu
. If

{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}(\mu)=+\infty
, then by (
2.5
) and Lemma

4.2
,
{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}(\mu)>0
. Thus, by Proposition
2.2
and
the definition of
\Gamma-
convergence,

\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\;\geq\;\lim_{\delta\to 0}\delta^{-1}\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\;\geq\;\lim_{\delta\to 0}\delta^{-1}{\mathscr{J}}^{(-1)}(\mu)\;=\;+\infty\,=\,{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}(\mu)\,.

This proves the
\Gamma-\liminf
for measures
\mu
such that
{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}(\mu)=+\infty
.

We turn to the case where
{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}(\mu)<+\infty
. Suppose
that

\mu(\cdot)=\sum_{z\in{\mathcal{C}}}\omega(z)\delta_{z}(\cdot),

for some
\omega\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathcal{C}})
. Without loss of generality, we may
also assume that

\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})<+\infty,

(4.4)

otherwise the result trivially holds. By the definition of the
rate functional
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
, for any function
F\in C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})

\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\,\geq\,\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}e^{F(x)/\epsilon}}{e^{F(x)/\epsilon}}d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\,.

By (
3.2
), and the definition of the functional
{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}
, it
is enough to find a sequence of test functions

F_{\epsilon}\in C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
, such that

\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\Big\{\,\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\big[\,{\textsf{b}}(x)+{\textsf{a}}(x)F^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x)\,\big]\,F^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x)\,+\,{\textsf{a}}(x)\,F^{\prime\prime}_{\epsilon}(x)\,\Big\}\,d\mu_{\epsilon}\,\geq\,\sum_{z\in{\mathcal{W}}}\omega(x)\,{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)\,.

(4.5)

To obtain the derivative
{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}
in the limit, for each

\sigma\in{\mathcal{W}}
we define a local version of the potential
S
with
the help of a cut-off function
\Phi_{\epsilon,\sigma}
whose support
is an interval containing
\sigma
, and no other critical point. The
test function is constructed as a linear combination of these local
potentials. Because of the factor
\epsilon^{-1}
appearing in the
first term of the integral in (
4.5
), and the
presence of the second derivative of the test function in the second
term of the same integral, the interval containing the support of the
derivative of the cut-off function needs to be carefully defined. Mind
that, by the definition of
{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}
, we can neglect the local
minima.

We now proceed to the construction of the test function. We start with
the construction of the intervals needed in the definition of the
cut-off function, see Figure
1
above. Fix

\sigma\in{\mathcal{W}}
. Choose four points

x_{\sigma,1}^{-},x_{\sigma,2}^{-},x_{\sigma,1}^{+},x_{\sigma,2}^{+}\in(0,1)
such that (a)

x_{\sigma,2}^{-}<x_{\sigma,1}^{-}<\sigma<x_{\sigma,1}^{+}<x_{\sigma,2}^{+}
, (b)
S(x_{\sigma,2}^{-})=S(x_{\sigma,2}^{+})
,

S(x_{\sigma,1}^{-})=S(x_{\sigma,1}^{+})
, and (c) the only critical
point in
[x_{\sigma,2}^{-},x_{\sigma,2}^{+}]
is
\sigma
:

[x_{\sigma,2}^{-},x_{\sigma,2}^{+}]\cap{\mathcal{C}}=\{\sigma\}\,.

(4.6)

Let
I_{\sigma}^{-},I_{\sigma}^{+}\subset(0,1)
be the intervals defined
by
I_{\sigma}^{-}:=[x_{\sigma,2}^{-},x_{\sigma,1}^{-}]
and

I_{\sigma}^{+}:=[x_{\sigma,1}^{+},x_{\sigma,2}^{+}]
. Since
\sigma

is a point of local maxima,
S
is strictly increasing on
I_{\sigma}^{-}

while strictly decreasing on
I_{\sigma}^{+}
. Then,

S(I_{\sigma}^{-})=S(I_{\sigma}^{+})
. Denote this interval by
J_{\sigma}
,

J_{\sigma}:=S(I_{\sigma}^{-})=S(I_{\sigma}^{+}).

(4.7)

By (
4.4
), (
4.6
) and Lemma

4.3
,

\mu_{\epsilon}(I_{\sigma}^{-}\cup I_{\epsilon}^{+})=O(\epsilon).

(4.8)

Partition the interval
J_{\sigma}
in
\lceil 1/\epsilon\rceil
sub
intervals of length
\lambda(J_{\sigma})/\lceil 1/\epsilon\rceil
,
where, recall,
\lambda(\cdot)
stands for the Lebesgue measure.
Denote these intervals by
J_{r,\epsilon}^{\sigma}
so that

J_{\sigma}=\bigsqcup_{r=1}^{\lceil 1/\epsilon\rceil}J_{r,\epsilon}^{\sigma}\,.

By (
4.7
) and (
4.8
), choose
some interval
J_{r,\epsilon}^{\sigma}
in the partition such that
