\mu_{\epsilon}\big(\,[\,I^{-}_{\sigma}\cup I^{+}_{\sigma}\,]\,\cap\,\Pi(S^{-1}(J_{r,\epsilon}^{\sigma}))\,\big)\,=\,O(\epsilon^{2}),\quad\lambda(J_{r,\epsilon}^{\sigma})=\lambda(J_{\sigma})/\lceil 1/\epsilon\rceil\,,

(4.9)

where
\Pi
is the projection defined in (
2.13
). Let

I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\subset I_{\sigma}^{-}
and

I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}\subset I_{\sigma}^{+}
be the intervals such that

I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}\,=\,(I^{-}_{\sigma}\cup I^{+}_{\sigma})\,\cap\,\Pi(S^{-1}(J_{r,\epsilon}^{\sigma}))\,.

By (
4.9
) and since the function
S(\cdot)
is
Lipschitz,

\mu_{\epsilon}(I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{\pm})=O(\epsilon^{2})\,,\quad\lambda(I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{\pm})=\Theta(\epsilon)\,,

(4.10)

where the notation
O(\cdot)
,
\Theta(\cdot)
has been introduced
just before the beginning of the proposition’s proof.
Let
y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{1,\pm}
,

y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{2,\pm}\in(0,1)
be the endpoints of the
intervals
I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{\pm}
,

I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}=[y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{2,-},y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{1,-}]
,

I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}=[y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{1,+},y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{2,+}]
, so that

x_{\sigma,2}^{-}<y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{2,-}<y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{1,-}<x_{\sigma,1}^{-}<\sigma<x_{\sigma,1}^{+}<y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{1,+}<y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{2,+}<x_{\sigma,2}^{+}\,.

(4.11)

Denote by
I_{\sigma,\epsilon}
the interval given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}I_{\sigma,\epsilon}}:=[y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{1,-},y_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{1,+}]
, and let
\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}
be some
nonnegative smooth function satisfying the following properties

(1)

\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}(x)=1
, for every

x\in I_{\sigma,\epsilon}
;

(2)

\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}
is strictly increasing on

I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}
while strictly decreasing on

I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}
;

(3)

\sup_{x\in I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}}|\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{\prime}(x)|=\Theta(\epsilon^{-1})
;

(4)

\sup_{x\in I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}}|\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{\prime\prime}(x)|=\Theta(\epsilon^{-2})
;
and

(5)

\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}(x)=0
, for every

x\in(I_{\sigma,\epsilon}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+})^{c}
.

Fix
c_{\sigma,\epsilon}\in J_{r,\epsilon}^{\sigma}
and let

S_{\sigma,\epsilon}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be the translated potential

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S_{\sigma,\epsilon}(x)}:=S(x)-c_{\sigma,\epsilon}
. By
(
4.9
),

\sup_{x\in I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}}|S_{\sigma,\epsilon}(x)|=O(\epsilon)\,.

(4.12)

Clearly, the product

\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}\cdot S_{\sigma,\epsilon}
defines an element
in
C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
. We perform the same construction for each local
maxima
\sigma\in{\mathcal{W}}
and let
F_{\epsilon}:{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be the
function

F_{\epsilon}(x):=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathcal{W}}}\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}(x)\,S_{\sigma,\epsilon}(x)\,.

(4.13)

By linearity,
F_{\epsilon}
is contained in the domain of

{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}
. It remains to show that the sequence
F_{\epsilon}

satisfies (
4.5
). By (
4.13
), the left
hand side of (
4.5
) is equal to

\displaystyle-\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathcal{W}}}\;\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\left\{\frac{{\textsf{b}}(x)\,+\,{\textsf{a}}(x)\left(\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}\cdot S_{\sigma,\epsilon}\right)^{\prime}(x)}{\epsilon}\right\}\left(\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}\cdot S_{\sigma,\epsilon}\right)^{\prime}(x)\;d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)

\displaystyle-\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathcal{W}}}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}(x)\left(\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}\cdot S_{\sigma,\epsilon}\right)^{\prime\prime}(x)\;d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\,.

(4.14)

We claim that the first sum of (
4
) vanishes as

\epsilon\to 0
, while the
\liminf
of the second sum is bounded below
by
\sum_{z\in{\mathcal{W}}}\omega(x){\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)
. We start with the first
sum. Fix
\sigma\in{\mathcal{W}}
. By item (1) in the definition of

\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}
and the fact that

S_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{\prime}=-{\textsf{b}}/{\textsf{a}}
, the expression inside the
brackets vanishes on
I_{\sigma,\epsilon}
. By item (5) in the
definition of
\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}
, the remaining factor vanishes
on

\big(I_{\sigma,\epsilon}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}\big)^{c}
. Then, it is enough to show that

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}}\left\{\frac{{\textsf{b}}(x)\,+\,{\textsf{a}}(x)\left(\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}\cdot S_{\sigma,\epsilon}\right)^{\prime}(x)}{\epsilon}\right\}\left(\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}\cdot S_{\sigma,\epsilon}\right)^{\prime}(x)\;d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\,=\,0\,.

Calculate the derivatives. By the triangle inequality, the previous
integral is bounded above by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon}\;\,\int_{I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}}\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,{\textsf{b}}(x)^{2}\,\big[\,1\,-\,\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}(x)\,\big]\,\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}(x)\,\;d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{\epsilon}\;\int_{I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}}\Big\{\,\big|\,{\textsf{b}}(x)\,\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{\prime}(x)\,\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}(x)\,S_{\sigma,\epsilon}(x)\,\big|\,+\,\big|\,{\textsf{b}}(x)\,\Phi^{\prime}(x)\,S_{\sigma,\epsilon}(x)\,\big|\,\Big\}\;d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\;\int_{I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}}{\textsf{a}}(x)\,\big[\,\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{\prime}(x)\,S_{\sigma,\epsilon}(x)\,\big]^{2}\;d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\,.

Since
{\textsf{b}},{\textsf{a}}
are bounded,
a
is strictly positive, and

0\leq\Phi\leq 1
, the previous expression is bounded above by

\frac{C_{0}}{\epsilon}\;\Big\{\mu_{\epsilon}(I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+})\,+\,\int_{I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}}\Big[\,\big|\,\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{\prime}(x)\,S_{\sigma,r(\epsilon)}(x)\,\big|\,+\,\big[\,\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{\prime}(x)\,S_{\sigma,r(\epsilon)}(x)\,\big]^{2}\,\Big]\;d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\,\Big\}

for some finite constant
C_{0}
. This expression is less than or equal
to

\displaystyle\frac{C_{0}}{\epsilon}\;\{\,1\,+a^{2}_{\epsilon}\}\,\mu_{\epsilon}(I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+})\;\;\text{where}\;\;a_{\epsilon}\,=\,\sup_{x\in I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}}|\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{\prime}(x)|\;\sup_{x\in I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}}|S_{\sigma,r(\epsilon)}(x)|\,.

By (
4.10
), (
4.12
) and item (3) in
the definition of
\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}
, this expression vanishes
as
\epsilon
goes to
0
. This proves that the first term in
(
4
) converges to
0
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

We turn to the second sum in (
4
). Fix

\sigma\in{\mathcal{W}}
. By the definitions of
\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}
and

S_{\sigma,\epsilon}
, the term in the sum corresponding to
\sigma

is equal to

\displaystyle\int_{I_{\sigma,\epsilon}}\Big\{\,{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(x)\,-\,\frac{{\textsf{b}}(x)\,{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}(x)}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,\Big\}\;d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\,-\,\int_{I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}}{\textsf{a}}(x)\,\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{\prime\prime}(x)\,S_{\sigma,\epsilon}(x)\;d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)

\displaystyle+\int_{I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}}\Big\{\,2\,\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{\prime}(x)\,{\textsf{b}}(x)\,+\,\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}(x)\,\Big[\,{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(x)-\frac{{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}(x)\,{\textsf{b}}(x)}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,\Big]\,\Big\}\;d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\,.

By items (3) and (4) in the definition of
\Phi_{\sigma,\epsilon}
,
(
4.10
), and (
4.12
), the integrals
over
I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{-}\cup I_{\sigma,\epsilon}^{+}
vanish as

\epsilon\to 0
. We turn to the integral over

I_{\sigma,\epsilon}
. Recall (
4.11
), let

I_{\sigma}:=[x_{\sigma,2}^{-},x_{\sigma,2}^{+}]
and rewrite the integral
as

\int_{I_{\sigma}}\Big\{\,{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(x)-\frac{{\textsf{b}}(x)\,{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}(x)}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,\Big\}\,d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)-\int_{I_{\sigma}\setminus I_{\sigma,\epsilon}}\Big\{\,{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(x)-\frac{{\textsf{b}}(x)\,{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}(x)}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,\Big\}\,d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\,.

By (
4
) and the assumption that
\mu_{\epsilon}
converges
weakly to
\mu
,

\mu_{\epsilon}(I_{\sigma}\setminus I_{\sigma,\epsilon})\to 0
as

\epsilon\to 0
. Hence, since the integrand is bounded, the second
integral in the previous displayed equation vanishes as

\epsilon\to 0
. To finish the proof, note that by (
4
)
and the fact that
{\textsf{b}}(\sigma)=0
, the first
integral converges to
\omega(\sigma)\,{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(\sigma)
, as

\epsilon\to 0
.
∎

Second order
\Gamma-\limsup

The main result of this subsection reads as follows.

Proposition 4.4
.

Assume that condition (H) is in force. Then, the functional

{\mathscr{J}}^{0}
is a
\Gamma-\limsup
for the sequence
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
.

The proof of this proposition requires some preparation. Fix a
probability measure
\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
. If

{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}(\mu)=+\infty
, there is nothing to prove. Hence, by
definition of
{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}(\cdot)
, we may assume that

\mu=\sum_{z\in{\mathcal{C}}}w(z)\,\delta_{z}
for some

w\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathcal{C}})
.

It is actually enough to prove that for every critical point

{\mathfrak{c}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
, there exists a sequence

\mu_{{\mathfrak{c}},\epsilon}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
which converges weakly to

\delta_{{\mathfrak{c}}}
and such that

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{{\mathfrak{c}},\epsilon})\leq{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}(\delta_{{\mathfrak{c}}})
. Indeed, in this case, setting

\nu_{\epsilon}:=\sum_{{\mathfrak{c}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\omega({\mathfrak{c}})\,\mu_{{\mathfrak{c}},\epsilon}
yields a sequence which converges weakly to

\mu=\sum_{z\in{\mathcal{C}}}w(z)\,\delta_{z}
and such that, by
convexity,

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\nu_{\epsilon})\,\leq\,\sum_{{\mathfrak{c}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\omega({\mathfrak{c}})\,\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{{\mathfrak{c}},\epsilon})\,\leq\,\sum_{{\mathfrak{c}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\omega({\mathfrak{c}})\,{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}(\delta_{{\mathfrak{c}}})\,=\,{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}(\mu)\,,

where the last inequality follows by (
2.5
).

Suppose, from now on, that
\mu=\delta_{{\mathfrak{c}}}
, for some

{\mathfrak{c}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
, and assume, without loss of generality, that

{\mathfrak{c}}=0
. To complete the proof of the
\Gamma-\limsup
, we need to
prove the following result.

Lemma 4.5
.

There exists a sequence of probability measures

\mu_{\epsilon}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
which converges weakly to

\delta_{0}
, and such that

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\;\leq\;b^{\prime}(0)\vee 0\,.

Fix
0<\delta_{0}<\delta_{1}<1/32
and let
\Phi\in C^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
be a
non-negative function, bounded by
1
, whose support is contained in

(-1/2+\delta_{0},1/2-\delta_{0})
and such that
\Phi(x)=1
for

x\in(-1/2+\delta_{1},1/2-\delta_{1})
. We also require
(\Phi^{\prime})^{2}/\Phi

to be bounded. This is not a serious condition. Let
\Psi
be a function
which fulfills all requirements for
\Phi
, but the last one. Define

\Phi
as
\Psi^{2}
. The first requirements hold for
\Phi
, and

(\Phi^{\prime})^{2}/\Phi=(\Psi^{\prime})^{2}
, which is a bounded function.

Let
G_{\epsilon}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
\epsilon>0
, be the function given by

G_{\epsilon}(x)=e^{-C\frac{x^{2}}{2\epsilon}},

where
C:=|{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(0)|/{\textsf{a}}(0)
. Consider the probability measure

\mu_{\epsilon}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
defined by

\mu_{\epsilon}(dx):=m_{\epsilon}(x)\,dx\,:=\,\frac{1}{Z_{\epsilon}}G_{\epsilon}(x)\Phi(x)\,dx\,,

where
Z_{\epsilon}
is a normalization constant that turns

\mu_{\epsilon}
a probability measure. Clearly, the support of the
measure
\mu_{\epsilon}
is contained in the interval
(-1/2,1/2)
, and

\mu_{\epsilon}
can be considered a probability measure on the
torus. Moreover, by a change of variables, as
\Phi(\cdot)
is
continuous at the origin and equal to
1
,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\,\frac{Z_{\epsilon}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\,=\,\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}e^{-C\frac{x^{2}}{2\epsilon}}\,\Phi(x)\,dx\,=\,\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{C}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}\,\Phi(x\sqrt{\epsilon}/\sqrt{C})\,dx\,=\,\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{C}}\,.

(4.15)

Assertion 4.6
.

The sequence of probability measures
(\mu_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}

converges weakly to
\delta_{0}
.

Proof.

Fix
\phi\in C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
. Since
\Phi
is bounded by one, a
change of variables yields

|\langle\mu_{\epsilon},\phi\rangle-\phi(0)|\leq\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{Z_{\epsilon}}\int_{-1/2\sqrt{\epsilon}}^{1/2\sqrt{\epsilon}}|\phi(\sqrt{\epsilon}x)-\phi(0)|\,e^{-C\frac{x^{2}}{2}}\,dx\,.

As
Z_{\epsilon}
is of order
\sqrt{\epsilon}
and
\phi
is
continuous, by the dominated convergence theorem, the right-hand
side of the previous expression vanishes as
\epsilon\to 0
, which
proves the assertion.
∎

To complete the proof of the lemma, we slightly perturb

\mu_{\epsilon}
in order to apply Lemma
3.2
to estimate

{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})
.

Assertion 4.7
.

Given
\delta>0
, let
\mu^{\delta}_{\epsilon}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
be the
probability measure given by the density

m_{\epsilon,\delta}(x):=\frac{m_{\epsilon}(x)+\delta}{1+\delta}.

Then,

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\left|\;{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon}^{\delta})\;-\;\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}\,\left\{\epsilon\Big[\frac{m_{\epsilon,\delta}^{\prime}}{m_{\epsilon,\delta}}\,+\,\frac{{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}}{{\textsf{a}}}\Big]\,-\,\frac{{\textsf{b}}}{{\textsf{a}}}\right\}^{2}d\mu_{\epsilon}^{\delta}\;\right|\;=\,0\,.

Proof.

Since
m_{\epsilon,\delta}(x)\geq\delta/(1+\delta)>0
,
x\in{\mathbb{T}}
,
by Lemma
3.2
,

{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu^{\delta}_{\epsilon})=\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}\,\left\{\epsilon\,\Big[\frac{m_{\epsilon,\delta}^{\prime}}{m_{\epsilon,\delta}}\,+\,\frac{{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}}{{\textsf{a}}}\Big]\,+\,\frac{B/({\textsf{a}}\,m_{\epsilon,\delta})}{\langle 1/{\textsf{a}}\,m_{\epsilon,\delta}\rangle}\,-\,\frac{{\textsf{b}}}{{\textsf{a}}}\right\}^{2}d\mu_{\epsilon}^{\delta}\,.

Expand the square. To get the desired result it is enough to show that
the integral of the terms involving the factor

B/({\textsf{a}}\,m_{\epsilon,\delta})
vanishes as
\delta\to 0
.

Since
m_{\epsilon}
vanishes on a set of strictly positive Lebesgue
measure, the term
\langle 1/{\textsf{a}}\,m_{\epsilon,\delta}\rangle
is bounded
below by
C_{0}\delta^{-1}
, and above by
C_{1}\delta^{-1}
for finite
constants
0<C_{0}<C_{1}<\infty
. Therefore, by the definitions of

\mu^{\delta}_{\epsilon}
and
B
,

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\,\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}\,\left\{\frac{B/({\textsf{a}}\,m_{\epsilon,\delta})}{\langle 1/{\textsf{a}}\,m_{\epsilon,\delta}\rangle}\right\}^{2}\mu^{(\delta)}_{\epsilon}\,=\,\lim_{\delta\to 0}\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\frac{B^{2}}{\langle 1/{\textsf{a}}\,m_{\epsilon,\delta}\rangle}=0\,,

and

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}-2{\textsf{b}}\,\frac{B/({\textsf{a}}\,m_{\epsilon,\delta})}{\langle 1/{\textsf{a}}\,m_{\epsilon,\delta}\rangle}d\mu^{(\delta)}_{\epsilon}(x)\,=\,\lim_{\delta\to 0}-\frac{B^{2}}{2\epsilon}\frac{1}{\langle 1/{\textsf{a}}\,m_{\epsilon,\delta}\rangle}\,=\,0\,.

On the other hand, since
m
and
a
are periodic,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\,\frac{B}{\langle 1/{\textsf{a}}\,m_{\epsilon,\delta}\rangle}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\Big[\frac{m_{\epsilon,\delta}^{\prime}(x)}{m_{\epsilon,\delta}}(x)\,+\,\frac{{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}(x)}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\Big]\,dx\,=\,0\,.

This completes the proof of the assertion.
∎

Since
\mu_{\epsilon}^{\delta}
converges weakly to
\mu_{\epsilon}
,
by the lower semicontinuity of
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
,

{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\,\leq\,\liminf_{\delta\to 0}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu^{\delta}_{\epsilon})\,.

(4.16)

Since
m^{\prime}_{\epsilon,\delta}=m^{\prime}_{\epsilon}/(1+\delta)
, by the
monotone convergence theorem and the previous assertion,

\liminf_{\delta\to 0}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu^{\delta}_{\epsilon})\,=\,\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}\,\left\{\epsilon\Big[\frac{m_{\epsilon}^{\prime}}{m_{\epsilon}}\,+\,\frac{{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}}{{\textsf{a}}}\Big]\,-\,\frac{{\textsf{b}}}{{\textsf{a}}}\right\}^{2}d\mu_{\epsilon}\,=\,\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}\,\left\{\epsilon\,\frac{m_{\epsilon}^{\prime}}{m_{\epsilon}}\,+\,\frac{\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}-{\textsf{b}}}{{\textsf{a}}}\,\right\}^{2}d\mu_{\epsilon}\,.

(4.17)

It remains to estimate the right-hand side as
\epsilon\to 0
.

We estimate term by term. We start with the cross term. We claim that

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\big[\,\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}(x)-{\textsf{b}}(x)\,\big]\,m^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x)\;dx\,=\,\frac{{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(0)}{2}\,\cdot

Indeed, as
\mu_{\epsilon}
converges to the Dirac mass at the origin,
an integration by parts yields the result.

In the second diagonal term, the expressions involving

{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}(\cdot)
are easy to estimate. Since
{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)
vanishes
at the critical points, and
{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
is strictly positive,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{4}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\left\{\epsilon\,\frac{{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}(x)^{2}}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,-\,2\,{\textsf{b}}(x)\,{\textsf{a}}^{\prime}(x)\right\}\,\mu_{\epsilon}(dx)\,=\,0\,.

The next two assertions take care of the remaining terms.

Assertion 4.8
.

With the notation introduced above,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}{\textsf{a}}\,\Big\{\epsilon\,\frac{m_{\epsilon}^{\prime}}{m_{\epsilon}}\,\Big\}^{2}d\mu_{\epsilon}\,=\,\frac{|{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(0)|}{4}\,\cdot

Proof.

By definition of
m_{\epsilon}
, and since
\Phi
has compact support
on
(-1/2,1/2)
, the left-hand side is equal to

\frac{\epsilon}{4\,Z_{\epsilon}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}{\textsf{a}}(x)\,\left\{-\,\frac{Cx}{\epsilon}\;+\;\frac{\Phi^{\prime}(x)}{\Phi(x)}\,\right\}^{2}\,\Phi(x)\,e^{-C\frac{x^{2}}{2\epsilon}}\;dx\,.

The cross term is easy to handle. After a change of variables it
becomes

-\,\frac{C\,\epsilon}{2\,Z_{\epsilon}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}{\textsf{a}}(\epsilon x)\,x\,\Phi^{\prime}(\epsilon x)\,e^{-Cx^{2}/2}\;dx\,.

Since
Z_{\epsilon}
is of order
\sqrt{\epsilon}
, this expression
vanishes as
\epsilon\to 0
.

On the other hand, since, by assumption,
\Phi^{\prime}(x)^{2}/\Phi(x)
is a
bounded function whose support is contained in

(-1/2+\delta_{0},-1/2+\delta_{1})\cup(1/2-\delta_{1},1/2-\delta_{0})

and
1/2-\delta_{1}\geq 1/4
, the second diagonal term is bounded by

C_{0}\,\frac{\epsilon}{Z_{\epsilon}}\,\int_{|x|\geq 1/4}{\textsf{a}}(x)\,\,e^{-Cx^{2}/2\epsilon}\;dx\,.

As
Z_{\epsilon}
is of order
\sqrt{\epsilon}
, this expression
vanishes as
\epsilon\to 0
.

We turn to the first diagonal term which is equal to

\frac{C^{2}}{4\,\epsilon\,Z_{\epsilon}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}{\textsf{a}}(x)\,x^{2}\,\Phi(x)\,e^{-Cx^{2}/2\epsilon}\;dx\,=\,\frac{C^{2}\sqrt{\epsilon}}{4\,Z_{\epsilon}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}{\textsf{a}}(\epsilon x)\,\Phi(\epsilon x)\,x^{2}\,e^{-Cx^{2}/2}\;dx\,.

Since
\Phi(\cdot)
,
{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
are continuous at the origin,

\Phi(0)=1
,
C=|{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(0)|/{\textsf{a}}(0)
, by (
4.15
) the previous
expression converges, as
\epsilon\to 0
, to
(1/4)|{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(0)|
. To
complete the proof of the assertion, it remains to recollect all
previous estimates.
∎

A similar argument yields that

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{{\textsf{b}}^{2}(x)}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,\mu_{\epsilon}(dx)\,=\,\frac{|{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(0)|}{4}\,\cdot

By (
4.16
), (
4.17
), and recollecting all previous estimates
yields that

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\leq\frac{{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(0)}{2}+\frac{|{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(0)|}{4}+\frac{|{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(0)|}{4}\,=\,{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(0)\vee 0\,.

We have established Lemma
4.5
, and, thus, also proven
Proposition
4.4
.

Proof of Proposition
2.3
.

The first assertion of the proposition is a direct consequence of
Propositions
4.1
and
4.4
. By the definition of the
functional
{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}
given in (
2.5
), (
2.6
),
the
0
-level set of the functional
{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}
consists of all
convex combinations of Dirac measures supported on the elements of

{\mathcal{M}}
.
∎

5.
Higher order expansion

In this section we introduce the main technical and theoretical tools
needed to prove Proposition
2.4
and Theorem
2.7
. The
presentation is divided in two parts.

In the first subsection, we introduce a useful representation formula
for the level two large deviation rate functionals
{\mathbb{I}}^{(p)}
,

1\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
, associated to the reduced Markov
chains
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}(\cdot)
defined in (
2.23
). This
formula, originally derived in
[
20
]
, will be used in Section

6
to verify the third condition in the definition of

\Gamma-
expansion, and in Section
7
to construct the
recovery sequence for the
\Gamma-\limsup
at the intermediate
time-scales.

In the second subsection, we state Proposition

5.1
, which was essentially proven in

[
27
]
. This result puts at our disposal the metastable properties
of the process
X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
that will be needed throughout the
remainder of the article. In particular, it plays a key role in
Section
6
, where it is used to construct the test functions
required for the proof of the
\Gamma-\liminf
of

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
, for

1\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
, and in Section
9
, where it is
used to define the densities of the recovery sequence needed to
establish the
\Gamma-\limsup
of

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
at the last metastable scale,
under the assumption that the reduced chain

\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\cdot)
is irreducible and non-reversible.

The rate function
{\mathbb{I}}^{(p)}
for the reduced Markov
chain
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}(\cdot)
:

Fix
1\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
and recall the definition of the rate
functional
{\mathbb{I}}^{(p)}
given in (
2.24
). In this subsection we
introduce a formula for
{\mathbb{I}}^{(p)}
that will be useful in the proof of
the
\Gamma-
convergence of
\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}{\mathscr{J}}_{\epsilon}
. This requires some preliminary notation.

For every set
{\mathfrak{C}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
, denote by

\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p,{\mathfrak{C}}}
the generator of the process

\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}(\cdot)
reflected on
{\mathfrak{C}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p,{\mathfrak{C}}}f)({\mathcal{M}})}=\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{C}}}R_{p}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})\{f({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})-f({\mathcal{M}})\}\,,\quad f:{\mathfrak{C}}\to\mathbb{R}\,,\quad{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}.

(5.1)

Similarly, given a set
{\mathfrak{C}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
, let

{\mathbb{I}}^{(p)}_{{\mathfrak{C}}}:{\textsf{P}}({\mathfrak{C}})\to[0,+\infty]
be the large
deviations rate functional associated to
\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p,{\mathfrak{C}}}

and given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{I}}^{(p)}_{{\mathfrak{C}}}(\omega^{\prime})}:=\sup_{h}-\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}}\frac{[\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p,{\mathfrak{C}}}h]({\mathcal{M}})}{h({\mathcal{M}})}\omega^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}),\hskip 14.22636pt\omega^{\prime}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathfrak{C}}),

(5.2)

where the supremum is taken with respect to all positive functions

h:{\mathfrak{C}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
.

Fix a probability measure
\omega\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{p})
. Let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{S}}_{p}^{(\omega)}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
be the support of

\omega
. We do not assume that
{\mathcal{S}}_{p}^{(\omega)}
is a proper
subset of
{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
. Denote by

\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}^{(\omega)}_{p}(\cdot)
the Markov chain
induced by
\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p,{\mathcal{S}}_{p}^{(\omega)}}
. Let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{E}}_{1}^{p},...,{\mathfrak{E}}_{o_{p}}^{p}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{p}^{(\omega)}
be the equivalence classes of

\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}^{(\omega)}(\cdot)
, and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}^{p},...,{\mathfrak{D}}_{l_{p}}^{p}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{p}^{(\omega)}
the equivalence classes of

\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}^{(\omega)}(\cdot)
containing two or more
elements. Here,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}o_{p}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}l_{p}}
are
nonnegative integers representing the number of equivalent classes and
equivalent classes with two or more elements, respectively.

Some elementary observations. If the support of
\omega(\cdot)
is
not a singleton and
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}^{(\omega)}
is irreducible,
then
o_{p}=l_{p}=1
, and
{\mathfrak{E}}_{1}^{p}={\mathfrak{D}}_{1}^{p}={\mathcal{S}}_{p}^{(\omega)}
. If
\omega
is strictly positive, then

\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}^{(\omega)}=\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}
. If, additionally,

\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}^{(\omega)}
is irreducible, then, by construction,

p=\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.

For every set
{\mathfrak{C}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
, let
\omega(\cdot|{\mathfrak{C}})
be the
measure
\omega
conditioned on
{\mathfrak{C}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\omega({\mathcal{M}}|{\mathfrak{C}})}:=\frac{\omega({\mathcal{M}})}{\omega({\mathfrak{C}})}\,,\quad{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathfrak{C}}.

Lemmata A.7 and A.9 in
[
20
]
provide the following
formula for the rate functional
{\mathbb{I}}^{(p)}
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{I}}^{(p)}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{l_{p}}\omega({\mathfrak{D}}^{p}_{r})\;{\mathbb{I}}^{(p)}_{{\mathfrak{D}}^{p}_{r}}(\omega(\;\cdot\;|\;{\mathfrak{D}}^{p}_{r}))\;+\;\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}^{(\omega)}}\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\neq{\mathcal{S}}_{p}^{(\omega)}}\omega({\mathcal{M}})R_{p}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\sum_{r=1}^{o_{p}}\sum_{s\neq r}\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathfrak{E}}^{p}_{r}}\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{E}}^{p}_{s}}\omega({\mathcal{M}})R_{p}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}).

(5.3)

Notice that for every
1\leq r\leq l_{p}
, the reflected generator

\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p,{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}^{p}}
is irreducible and the measure

\omega(\cdot|{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}^{p})
, is strictly positive on
{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}^{p}
.
Denote by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\nu_{{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}}^{p}}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathfrak{D}}_{r})
the
stationary state of the Markov chain induced by

\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p,{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}}
. By Lemma A.3 in
[
20
]
, for every

1\leq r\leq l_{p}
there exists a strictly positive function

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{h}_{r}\colon{\mathfrak{D}}^{p}_{r}\to{\mathbb{R}}}
, unique up to
a multiplicative constant, such that

{\mathbb{I}}^{(p)}_{{\mathfrak{D}}^{p}_{r}}(\omega(\cdot|{\mathfrak{D}}^{p}_{r}))=-\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathfrak{D}}^{p}_{r}}\frac{[\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p,{\mathfrak{D}}^{p}_{r}}\;\widehat{h}_{r}]({\mathcal{M}})}{\widehat{h}_{r}({\mathcal{M}})}\;\omega({\mathcal{M}}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{p}_{r})\,.

(5.4)

Denote by
\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p,\widehat{h}_{r}}
the reflected generator

\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p,{\mathfrak{D}}^{p}_{r}}
tilted by the function

\widehat{h}_{r}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p,\widehat{h}_{r}}f)({\mathcal{M}})}:=\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}^{p}}\frac{R_{p}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})\,\widehat{h}_{r}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})}{\widehat{h}_{r}({\mathcal{M}})}\{f({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})-f({\mathcal{M}})\}\,,

(5.5)

for
f\colon{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}^{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Let

R_{p,\widehat{h}_{r}}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})\,:=\,\widehat{h}_{r}({\mathcal{M}})^{-1}\,R_{p}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})\,\widehat{h}_{r}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})
be the associated tilted jump-rates. Clearly, the Markov chain
induced by the tilted generator
\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p,\widehat{h}_{r}}
is
irreducible as well. By
[
20
, Lemma A.2]
, the probability
measure
\omega(\cdot|{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}^{p})
is the unique stationary state for
the tilted generator
\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p,\widehat{h}_{r}}
:

\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}^{p}}(\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p,\widehat{h}_{r}})f({\mathcal{M}})\,\omega({\mathcal{M}}|{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}^{p})=0\,,

(5.6)

for every
f\colon{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}^{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
.

If the measure
\omega(\cdot\,|{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}^{p})
satisfies the detailed
balance conditions for the jump rates
R_{p,\widehat{h}_{r}}
, by Lemma

B.1
and Theorem 5 in
[
9
]
the function

\widehat{h}_{r}
is given by

\widehat{h}_{r}({\mathcal{M}}):=\sqrt{\frac{\omega({\mathcal{M}}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{p}_{r})}{\nu^{p}_{{\mathfrak{D}}^{p}_{r}}({\mathcal{M}})}}\,,

where, recall,
\nu_{{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}}^{p}
is the
stationary state of the Markov chain induced by

\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p,{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}}
.

Resolvent characterization of metastability

In this subsection, we introduce the main tool needed for the proof of
the
\Gamma-
convergence of
\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
,

1\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.

Denote by
N(x,r)
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
r>0
, the connected
component of the set
\{y\in{\mathbb{R}}:S(y)<S(x)+r\}
that contains
x
,
and by
B(x,r)
the open ball of radius
r
centered at

x
. Fix
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{r}}>0
such that

B(m_{k},\hat{r})\subsetneq(0,1)
for every
0\leq k\leq N-1
. Recall
the definition of the height
{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
introduced in (
2.10
). Given

0<r<{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\wedge\hat{r}
, denote by

{\mathcal{N}}_{r}(m_{k})\subset{\mathbb{T}}
,
0\leq k\leq N-1
, the set defined by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{N}}_{r}(m_{k})}:=\Pi(N(x,r)\cap B(m_{k},r)).

Fix
0<{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r_{0}}<(1/2)\,({\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\wedge\hat{r})
,
and denote by
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(m_{k})
,
0\leq k\leq N-1
, the set given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(m_{k})}:={\mathcal{N}}_{r_{0}}(m_{k}).

(5.7)

For each
m_{k}\in{\mathcal{M}}
we say that
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(m_{k})
is a first order
well. By the definition of
r_{0}
,

{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(m_{k})\cap{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(m_{l})\,=\,\varnothing,

(5.8)

for
k,l\in S_{1}
,
k\neq l
.

Fix
1\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
, and recall from condition (c) above equation
(
2.15
) that
{\mathfrak{u}}_{p}
represents the number of elements of

{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
. Let
S_{p}=\{0,\dots,{\mathfrak{u}}_{p}-1\}
. Define the

p-
order well
{\mathcal{E}}_{p}(k)
,
k\in S_{p}
, by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{E}}_{p}(k)}:=\bigcup_{m\in{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)}{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(m).

Note that the set
{\mathcal{E}}_{p}(k)
is disconnected if
{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)
is not
a singleton. By property
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(p)
in Appendix
A
,
(
5.8
) and the definition of
r_{0}
, we also have

{\mathcal{E}}_{p}(k)\cap{\mathcal{E}}_{p}(l)=\varnothing
, for
k,l\in S_{p}
,

k\neq l
. Note that we can induce on
\{{\mathcal{E}}_{p}(k)\}_{k\in S_{p}}
the
order given in
{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
. Fix
r\in(2r_{0},{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\wedge\hat{r})
,
and for every
k\in S_{p}
, choose a smooth function

\zeta^{p,k}\colon{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that

{\textsf{1}}\{{\mathcal{E}}_{p}(k)\}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\zeta^{p,k}}\leq{\textsf{1}}\{{\mathcal{N}}^{p}_{r}(k)\}\,,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{N}}^{p}_{r}(k)}:=\bigcup_{{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)}{\mathcal{N}}_{r}({\mathfrak{m}}).

(5.9)

Fix a function
g\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, and let

G_{g}:{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be its smooth lifting to
{\mathbb{T}}
given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}G_{g}(x)}=\sum_{k\in S_{p}}g({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))\,\zeta_{p,k}(x).

(5.10)

Note that
G_{g}
is smooth, constant on each well
{\mathcal{E}}_{p}(k)
,

k\in S_{p}
, and vanishes on

{\mathbb{T}}\setminus\bigcup_{k\in S_{p}}{\mathcal{N}}_{r}(k)
. For
\lambda>0
, let

\phi_{p,\epsilon}
be the unique solution of the resolvent equation

(\lambda-\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon})\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\phi_{p,\epsilon}}=G_{g}.

(5.11)

Since
G_{g}
is smooth, the function
\phi_{p,\epsilon}
belongs to the
domain of
{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}
. In particular, it is of class

C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
. We are now in a position to state the main tool in the
proof of the
\Gamma-
convergence of the rate functional

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
.

Proposition 5.1
.

For every
1\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
,
\lambda>0
, and

g\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, the unique solution
\phi_{p,\epsilon}
of
the resolvent equation (
5.11
) satisfies

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\max_{k\in S_{p}}\sup_{x\in{\mathscr{E}}_{p}(k)}|\phi_{p,\epsilon}(x)-f_{p}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))|=0,

(5.12)

where
f_{p}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is the unique solution of the
reduced resolvent equation

(\lambda-\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p})f_{p}=g.

Proof.

Fix
1\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
,
\lambda>0
and a function

g\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
G^{*}\colon{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be the function
defined by

G^{*}(x)=\sum_{k\in S_{p}}g({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))1_{{\mathcal{E}}_{p}(k)}(x).

Denote by
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}
the solution of the resolvent equation
(
5.11
) with
G^{*}
on the right hand side. The
same argument given in the proof of
[
27
, Theorem 3.3]
, yields

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\max_{k\in S_{p}}\sup_{x\in{\mathscr{E}}_{p}(k)}|\phi_{p,\epsilon}^{*}(x)-f_{p}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))|=0.

To finish the proof, note that
[
31
, Proposition 4.2]
and the
above equation, imply (
5.12
).
∎

Remark 5.2
.

For a discussion on the connection between the resolvent condition and
the metastable behaviour of Markov processes, we refer the reader to

[
28
]
for the case of Markov chains on countable state
spaces, and
[
31
]
for the case of metastable diffusions.

We finish this subsection with three more properties of the solution of
the resolvent equation (
5.11
). Recall that
{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{\epsilon}
denotes the law of the diffusion
X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
, starting from
x\in{\mathbb{T}}
, and write
{\mathbb{E}}_{x}^{\epsilon}
for the corresponding expectation. Similarly, let

{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}
be the law of the re-scaled
diffusion

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}X_{\epsilon}^{(p)}(t)}:=X_{\epsilon}(\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}t)
,
t\geq 0
, starting from

x\in{\mathbb{T}}
, and denote by
{\mathbb{E}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}
the
associated expectation. Clearly,

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}
is the infinitesimal generator
of
X_{\epsilon}^{(p)}(\cdot)
. Then, by
[
12
, Section 6.5]
, the
solution of (
5.11
) admits the following
stochastic representation:

\phi_{p,\epsilon}(x)={\mathbb{E}}^{p,\epsilon}_{x}\Big[\,\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda s}\,G_{g}(X_{\epsilon}^{(p)}(s))\;ds\,\Big]\;.

The following result is a simple consequence of this formula.

Lemma 5.3
.

Fix
\lambda>0
,
1\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
and a bounded function

g:{\mathcal{S}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
\phi_{\epsilon}
be the solution of
(
5.11
). Then,

\sup_{\epsilon>0}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}}|\phi_{\epsilon}(x)|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}}|G_{g}(x)|<+\infty.

Moreover, if
g
is nonnegative, then
\phi_{\epsilon}
is also
nonnegative.

The next result provides a representation of the solution of the
resolvent equation (
5.11
) in terms of level

p
-minima hitting
times

For every closed set
A\subset{\mathbb{T}}
, let
\tau(A)
be the first time
the trajectory
{\mathtt{x}}(\cdot)
reaches the set
A
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\tau(A)}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:{\mathtt{x}}(t)\in A\}\,.

If
A
is a singleton or a pair, say
A=\{a\}
,
A=\{a,b\}
for some

a,b\in{\mathbb{T}}
, we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\tau(a)}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\tau(a,b)}
for

\tau(\{a\})
,
\tau(\{a,b\})
, respectively.

Fixed a level
1\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
, let
{\mathcal{M}}_{p}\subset{\mathcal{M}}
be the set of local minima of level
p
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{M}}_{p}}:=\bigcup_{k\in S_{p}}{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)\,.

For each point

x\in{\mathbb{T}}\setminus{\mathcal{M}}_{p}
, which is not a local minima of level

p
, denote by
m_{p}^{-}(x),m_{p}^{+}(x)
the closest level

p
minima to the left, right of
x
, respectively. That is,

m_{p}^{-}(x)
,
m_{p}^{+}(x)\in{\mathcal{M}}_{p}
and

x\in(\,m_{p}^{-}(x),\,m_{p}^{+}(x)\,)\,,\quad[\,m_{p}^{-}(x),\,m_{p}^{+}(x)\,]\,\cap\,{\mathcal{M}}_{p}\,=\,\{\,m_{p}^{-}(x),\,m_{p}^{+}(x)\,\}\,.

Lemma 5.4
.

Fix
\lambda>0
,
1\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
and a bounded function

g\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
\phi_{p,\epsilon}
be the solution of
(
5.11
). Then, for every

x\in{\mathbb{T}}\setminus{\mathcal{M}}_{p}
,

\displaystyle\Big|\,\phi_{p,\epsilon}(x)\,-\,\phi_{p,\epsilon}(m_{p}^{-}(x))\,{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{\epsilon}\big[\,\tau(m_{p}^{-}(x))<\tau(m_{p}^{+}(x))\,\big]\,-\,\phi_{p,\epsilon}(m_{p}^{+}(x))\,{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{\epsilon}\big[\,\tau(m_{p}^{+}(x))<\tau(m_{p}^{-}(x))\,\big]\,\Big|

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2\,\|G_{g}\|_{\infty}\,{\mathbb{E}}_{x}^{\epsilon}\Big[\tau(m_{p}^{-}(x),m_{p}^{+}(x))\,/\,\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\Big]\,.

In particular,

\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\inf_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}}\phi_{p,\epsilon}(x)\,\geq\,\min_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}f_{p}({\mathcal{M}})\,.

Proof.

The first assertion of the lemma follows from
[
27
, Proposition
4.1]
. We turn to the second. Fix
x\in{\mathbb{T}}\setminus{\mathcal{M}}_{p}
. By
the first assertion of the lemma,

\displaystyle\phi_{p,\epsilon}(x)\,\geq\,\min_{m\in{\mathcal{M}}_{p}}\phi_{p,\epsilon}(m)\,-\,2\,\|G_{g}\|_{\infty}\,{\mathbb{E}}_{x}^{\epsilon}\Big[\tau(m_{p}^{-}(x),m_{p}^{+}(x))\,/\,\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\Big]\,.

By Lemma
C.1
, the previous expression is bounded below by

\min_{m\in{\mathcal{M}}_{p}}\phi_{p,\epsilon}(m)\,-\,\frac{C}{\epsilon}e^{[h_{p-1}-h_{p}]/\epsilon}\,,

for some finite constant
C
which doesn’t depend on
\epsilon
nor on

x
. Here, we adopted the convention that
h_{0}=0
. Since
{\mathcal{M}}_{p}

is a finite set and
h_{p}>h_{p-1}
, to complete the proof it remains to
let
\epsilon\to 0
and to recall the statement of Proposition

5.1
.
∎

6.
\Gamma-\liminf
in the metastable time-scales

In this subsection we prove that
{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}
is a
\Gamma-\liminf

of
\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
for

1\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
. This is the content of Proposition

6.2
. The core of the proof resides in the case where

{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}
is finite, since the remaining situation will trivially
follow by the definition of a
\Gamma-\liminf
. The proof is divided
in two parts. First, we use (
5
) and
(
5.4
) to show that, for
2\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
, the
functional
{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}(\mu)
is finite if, and only if,
\mu
belongs
to the zero-level set of
{\mathscr{J}}^{(p-1)}
. This verifies property (3)
in Definition
2.1
. Next, we use the resolvent
characterization of metastability to construct a test function that
yield the desired
\Gamma-\liminf
bound.

Fix
1\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
and recall the definition of the metastable
states
\mu^{p}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)}(\cdot)
,
k\in S_{p}
, introduced in
(
2.25
). Denote by
{\textsf{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{p}}\subset{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})

the convex-hull of the
p-
order metastable states:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\textsf{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{p}}}:=\Big\{\sum_{l\in S_{p}}\omega({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(l))\,\mu^{p}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(l)}(\cdot):\;\omega\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathscr{S}}_{p})\Big\}\,.

The next result characterizes the
0-
level set of
{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}
in
terms of
{\textsf{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{p+1}}
.

Proposition 6.1
.

For every
0\leq p<\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
, the
0-
level set of
{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}
is the
convex hull of the
(p+1)-
order metastable states. That is,

\left\{\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}}):{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}(\mu)=0\right\}\,=\,{\textsf{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{p+1}}.

Proof.

For
p=0
, the result follows directly by the definitions of

{\mathscr{J}}^{(0)}
,
\mu^{1}_{m_{k}}
,
k\in S_{1}
, given in (
2.5
),
(
2.25
), respectively.

We turn to the case where
1\leq p<\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
. Fix

\mu\in{\textsf{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{p+1}}
, and choose

\omega_{p+1}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{p+1})
such that

\mu(\cdot)=\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}\omega_{p+1}({\mathscr{M}})\,\mu_{{\mathcal{M}}}^{p+1}(\cdot).

(6.1)

Let
\omega_{p}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{p})
be the measure given by

\omega_{p}({\mathcal{M}})\>:=\>\begin{cases}\omega_{p+1}({\mathcal{M}}_{p+1}(k))\,\nu^{p}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{p+1}(k)}({\mathcal{M}}),&\text{if }{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{p+1}(k),\text{ for }k\in S_{p+1}\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\ ,\end{cases}

where
\nu^{p}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{p+1}(k)}(\cdot)
,
k\in S_{p+1}
, is the measure
introduced in (
B.1
). By (
A.1
) and Proposition

B.2
,

\mu(\cdot)=\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\omega_{p}({\mathcal{M}})\,\mu_{{\mathcal{M}}}^{p}(\cdot).

By (
6.1
) and (
A.1
), every closed irreducible
class of
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}^{(\omega_{p})}_{p}(\cdot)
that is contained in
the support of
\omega_{p}
is a closed irreducible class of

\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}
. Moreover,
\omega_{p}({\mathcal{M}})=0
for every

\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}(\cdot)-
transient state
{\mathcal{M}}
. Then, since

\nu_{{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}^{p}}(\cdot)
is the stationary state of the Markov chain
induced by
\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p,{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}^{p}}
,
1\leq r\leq l_{p}
, by
(
2.26
) and (
5
),

{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}(\mu)\,=\,{\mathbb{I}}^{(p)}(\omega_{p})\,=\,\sum_{r=1}^{l_{p}}{\mathbb{I}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}^{p}}^{(p)}(\nu_{{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}^{p}}^{p})\,=\,0.

We turn to the converse. Fix
\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
and assume that

{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}(\mu)=0
. By the definition of
{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}
,

\mu(\cdot)=\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\omega({\mathcal{M}})\mu_{{\mathscr{M}}}^{p}(\cdot)
, for some
\omega\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{p})
. By
(
5
),

\displaystyle 0={\mathbb{I}}^{(p)}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{l_{p}}\omega({\mathfrak{D}}^{p}_{r})\;{\mathbb{I}}^{(p)}_{{\mathfrak{D}}^{p}_{r}}(\omega(\;\cdot\;|\;{\mathfrak{D}}^{p}_{r}))\;+\;\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}^{(\omega)}}\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\neq{\mathscr{S}}_{p}^{(\omega)}}\omega({\mathcal{M}})R_{p}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\sum_{r=1}^{o_{p}}\sum_{s\neq r}\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathfrak{E}}^{p}_{r}}\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{E}}^{p}_{s}}\omega({\mathcal{M}})R_{p}({\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}).

(6.2)

Since each sum on the right-hand side of the above expression is
nonnegative, each sum must be equal to
0
. Let
{\mathfrak{D}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{p}

be an equivalent class of
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}(\cdot)
such that

{\mathfrak{D}}\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{p}^{(\omega)}\neq\varnothing
. Since the second sum
on the right-hand side of (
6
) vanishes,
{\mathfrak{D}}
is
completely contained in the support of
\omega
,

{\mathfrak{D}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{p}^{(\omega)}
. As the third sum is equal to
0
,

{\mathcal{S}}_{p}^{(\omega)}
cannot contain transient states of

\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}(\cdot)
. Thus,
{\mathfrak{D}}
must be a closed irreducible
class of
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}(\cdot)
. Moreover, as the first sum also
vanishes,
\omega({\mathcal{M}})\,=\,c_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\,\nu_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}})
,

{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathfrak{D}}
, where, recall,
\nu_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
is the stationary state
of
\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p,{\mathfrak{D}}}
, and
c_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
is some positive
constant that depends only on
{\mathfrak{D}}
. Clearly,

\omega({\mathfrak{D}})=c_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
so that

\omega({\mathcal{M}})\,=\ \omega({\mathfrak{D}})\,\nu_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}})\,,\quad{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathfrak{D}}\,.

(6.3)

To finish the proof, let

\omega_{p+1}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{p+1})
be the measure defined by

\omega_{p+1}({\mathcal{M}}_{p+1}(k))=\omega({\mathcal{M}}_{p+1}(k))\,,\quad k\in S_{p+1}\,.

By definition of the mesure
\mu(\cdot)
, since
\omega({\mathcal{M}})=0
if

{\mathcal{M}}
does not belong to a closed irreducible class of

\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}(\cdot)
, and by (
6.3
),

\displaystyle\mu(\cdot)\,=\,\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\omega({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})\,\mu_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}}^{p}(\cdot)\,

\displaystyle=\,\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{p+1}(k)}\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}:{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{M}}}\omega({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})\,\mu_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}}^{p}(\cdot)

\displaystyle=\,\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{p+1}(k)}\omega({\mathcal{M}})\,\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}:{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{M}}}\nu_{{\mathcal{M}}}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})\,\mu_{{\mathcal{M}}}^{p}(\cdot)\,.

By Proposition
B.2
and the definition of

\omega_{p+1}(\cdot)
, this expression is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}\omega_{p+1}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})\,\mu_{{\mathscr{M}}^{\prime}}^{p+1}(\cdot),

which completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

We are now in position to prove the main result of this subsection.

Proposition 6.2
.

For every
0\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
, the functional
{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}
, defined
in (
2.5
), (
2.14
), (
2.26
), is a
\Gamma-\liminf
of

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
p
. The case
p=0
is the content of
Proposition
4.1
. Fix
1\leq p\leq\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
, and assume that

{\mathscr{J}}^{(q)}
is a
\Gamma-\liminf
of

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(q)}{\mathscr{J}}_{\epsilon}
for every
0\leq q<p
. Fix a
measure
\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
and a sequence

(\mu_{\epsilon}:\epsilon>0)\subset{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
such that

\mu_{\epsilon}
converges weakly to
\mu
. If

\mu\notin{\textsf{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{p}}
, then by Proposition

6.1
,
{\mathscr{J}}^{(p-1)}(\mu)>0
. Thus, by the
inductive hypothesis and (
2.20
),

+\infty=\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{\theta^{p+1}}{\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}}{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}(\mu)\leq\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{\theta^{p+1}}{\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}}\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\leq\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p+1)}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon}).

Hence,

{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}(\mu)\leq\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p+1)}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})
, if
\mu

isn’t a convex combination of
p-
order metastable states.

We turn to the case where
\mu\in{\textsf{P}}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{p}}
. Choose

\omega\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathcal{C}})
such that

\mu(\cdot)=\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\omega({\mathcal{M}})\mu^{p}_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\cdot).

Fix a strictly positive function
h:{\mathcal{S}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Let

h_{0}:=\min_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}h({\mathcal{M}})
, choose a finite constant

\lambda
such that

\lambda>\max_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}|(\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p}h)({\mathcal{M}})|/h_{0}
, and
let
H\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be the function given by

H({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)):=[(\lambda-\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p})h]({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)),\hskip 14.22636pt\forall k\in S_{p}.

Note that
H
is strictly positive. Let

\Phi_{\epsilon}^{h}:{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be the solution of the
resolvent equation (
5.11
) with
G:=G_{H}
on the
right-hand side, where
G_{H}
is given by (
5.10
). Hence,

\Phi_{\epsilon}^{h}\in C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
and, by Lemma

5.3
,
\Phi^{h}_{\epsilon}
is nonnegative. Let

\delta>0
be an arbitrary positive constant and let

\Phi^{h,\delta}_{\epsilon}:{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be the function given by

\Phi^{h,\delta}_{\epsilon}(x):=\Phi^{h}_{\epsilon}(x)+\delta.

Then,
\Phi^{h,\delta}_{\epsilon}
is a positive function of class

C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
. Thus, by the definitions of
{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
and
H
,

\displaystyle\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})

\displaystyle\geq-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{[\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}\Phi_{\epsilon}^{h,\delta}](x)}{\Phi_{\epsilon}^{h,\delta}(x)}d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)=-\sum_{k\in S_{p}}[(\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p})h]({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))\int_{{\mathscr{N}}^{p}_{r}(k)}\frac{\zeta^{p,k}(x)}{\Phi^{h,\delta}_{\epsilon}(x)}d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)

(6.4)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k\in S_{p}}\int_{{\mathcal{N}}^{p}_{r}(k)}\frac{\lambda\{h({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\zeta^{p,k}(x)-\Phi_{\epsilon}^{h,\delta}(x)\}}{\Phi^{h,\delta}_{\epsilon}(x)}d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}\setminus\{\bigcup_{k\in S_{p}}{\mathscr{N}}^{p}_{r}(k)\}}\frac{\lambda\Phi_{\epsilon}^{h,\delta}(x)}{\Phi_{\epsilon}^{h,\delta}(x)}d\mu_{\epsilon}(x).

Since
\mu_{\epsilon}
converges weakly to
\mu
, and
\mu
is
concentrated on the set
{\mathcal{M}}_{p}\subset\bigcup_{k\in S_{p}}{\mathcal{N}}^{p}_{r}(k)
,
the third term on the right hand side of (
6.4
) vanishes as

\epsilon\to 0
. We turn to the second term. By the definition of

\Phi^{h,\delta}_{\epsilon}
, we can write this expression as

\sum_{k\in S_{p}}\int_{{\mathcal{N}}^{p}_{r}(k)}\frac{\lambda\{h({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\zeta^{p,k}(x)-\Phi_{\epsilon}^{h}(x)\}}{\Phi^{h,\delta}_{\epsilon}(x)}d\mu_{\epsilon}(x)-\int_{{\mathcal{N}}^{p}_{r}(k)}\frac{\lambda\delta}{\Phi^{h}_{\epsilon}(x)+\delta}d\mu_{\epsilon}(x).

(6.5)

By Proposition
5.1
, by the definition of

\zeta^{p,k}
given in (
5.9
), and the assumption that

\mu_{\epsilon}
converges weakly to
\mu
, the first term in
(
6.5
) vanishes as
\epsilon\to 0
. Similarly, the second one
converges to

\sum_{k\in S_{p}}\frac{\lambda\delta}{h({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))+\delta}\,\mu({\mathcal{E}}^{p}(k))\,.

By (
2.25
),
\mu^{p}_{{\mathcal{M}}}({\mathcal{M}})=1
for all
{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathscr{S}}_{p}
. Thus, by definition of
\mu
,

\mu({\mathcal{E}}^{p}(k))=\mu({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))=\omega({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))
. Hence, the
previous sum is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in S_{p}}\frac{\lambda\delta}{h({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))+\delta}\,\omega({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)).

Similarly, by Proposition
5.1
and
(
5.9
), the first term on the right hand side of
(
6.4
) converges to

-\sum_{k\in S_{p}}\frac{[\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p}h]({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))}{h({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))+\delta}\;\mu({\mathcal{E}}^{p}(k))=-\sum_{k\in S_{p}}\frac{[\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p}h]({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))}{h({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))+\delta}\;\omega({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)),

Recollecting the previous estimates one obtains

\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\geq-\sum_{k\in S_{p}}\frac{[\widehat{{\mathbb{L}}}_{p}h]({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))}{h({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))+\delta}\;\omega({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))-\sum_{k\in S_{p}}\frac{\lambda\delta}{h({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))+\delta}\omega({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)).

By (
2.24
), sending
\delta\to 0
first and then taking the
supremum over all positive functions
h\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
yields that

\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\geq{\mathbb{I}}^{(p)}(\omega)\,.

This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

7.
The
\Gamma-\limsup
in the time-scales

\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}
,
1\leq p<\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}

In this section, we prove the
\Gamma-\limsup
for all metastable time
scales with the exception of the last
one. The main result reads as follows.

Proposition 7.1
.

For every
1\leq p<\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
, the functional
{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}
, defined
in (
2.14
), (
2.26
), is a
\Gamma-\limsup
of

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}
.

Fix
1\leq p<\widehat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
. Since
p
is fixed, most of the time it is
omitted from the notation. For every
\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
such that

{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}(\mu)=\infty
, and any sequence
\mu_{\epsilon}
weakly
converging to
\mu
, the bound

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{\epsilon})\,\leq\,{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}(\mu)

holds trivially. Hence, we may assume that
{\mathscr{J}}^{(p)}(\mu)
is
finite. In view of (
2.26
), this means that it is enough to
consider measures
\mu\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
of the form

\mu(\cdot)\,=\,\sum_{k\in S_{p}}\omega({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))\,\mu^{p}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)}(\cdot)

(7.1)

for some
\omega\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{p})
, where

\mu^{p}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)}(\cdot),
is the measure introduced in
(
2.25
). By Lemma B5 in
[
20
]
, we can assume that

\omega({\mathcal{M}})>0
for every
{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
. In the next
subsections, we construct a recovery sequence for
\mu
.

The recovery sequence
(\mu_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
constructed below
is a convex combination of measures
\mu_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
supported
on neighborhoods of the equivalence classes
{\mathfrak{D}}
of

\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}
. For each
{\mathfrak{D}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
, the measure

\mu_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
is absolutely continuous, with density

m_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
given by the product of a localized version of
the Gibbs density

\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\exp\{-S(x)/\epsilon\}

and the square of a function
J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
. The function

J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
is defined as the harmonic extension of a
function whose values on neighborhoods of each
{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathfrak{D}}
are
prescribed by the solution of the variational problem (
5.2
) for

{\mathbb{I}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}^{(p)}
.

\sigma_{k-1,k}^{1}
\sigma_{k,k+1}^{1}
{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k)
\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
B_{k-1,k}^{1.-}
\Lambda_{k-1,k}^{1}
B_{k-1,k}^{1,+}
C_{k-1,k}^{1,2}
{\mathcal{E}}(m_{k})
B_{k,k+1}^{1,-}
\Lambda_{k,k+1}^{1}
B_{k,k+1}^{1,+}

Figure 2.
We illustrate an example of the intervals defined in
(
7.12
), (
7.13
), (
7.15
) and
(
7.17
) for the first metastable level
p=1
. Here,

{\mathfrak{D}}
is a
{\mathbb{X}}_{1}(\cdot)-
equivalent class composed of only one
state,
{\mathscr{D}}={\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k)
.

\sigma_{{\mathfrak{D}}}^{-}
\sigma_{{\mathfrak{D}}}^{+}
\sigma_{k,k+1}^{p,1}
\sigma_{k,k+1}^{p,2}
\sigma_{k,k+1}^{p,3}
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k-1)
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k+1)
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k+2)
\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}^{p}

Figure 3.
We illustrate the definition of the test function for a
higher-order metastable level
p\geq 2
. In this picture,
{\mathfrak{D}}
is a
closed irreducible class of
{\mathbb{X}}_{p}(\cdot)
, which is composed by the
states
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
and
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k+1)
. The set of maxima

{\mathscr{W}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
, associated to
{\mathfrak{D}}
, is equal to

\{\sigma_{{\mathfrak{D}}}^{-},\sigma_{k,k+1}^{1},\sigma_{k,k+1}^{2},\sigma_{k,k+1}^{3},\sigma_{{\mathfrak{D}}}^{+}\}
. Here, the maxima

\sigma_{{\mathfrak{D}}}^{-}
is the leftmost separating
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k-1)
and

{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
, while
\sigma_{{\mathfrak{D}}}^{+}
is the rightmost maxima
separating
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k+1)
and
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k+2)
. Since the set

{\mathscr{W}}_{k-1,k}
is a singleton,

\sigma_{{\mathfrak{D}}}^{-}=\sigma_{k-1,k}^{-}=\sigma_{k-1,k}^{+}
.
Similarly, since
{\mathscr{W}}_{k+1,k+2}
is also a singleton,

\sigma_{{\mathfrak{D}}}^{+}=\sigma_{k+1,k+2}^{+}=\sigma_{k+1,k+2}^{-}=\sigma_{k,k+1}^{{\mathfrak{r}}_{k,k+1}+1}
. The sets
\Lambda^{r}_{k-1,k}

defined in (
7.13
), are represented in green. The binding
sets, defined in (
7.17
), are the ones in red. The
intervals in brown, blue, yellow and pink represent the sets

C^{r}_{k-1,k}
defined in (
7.15
). The set
\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
,
represented in violet, is the neighborhood of
{\mathfrak{D}}
defined in
(
7.12
). The test function
J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
vanishes outside of

\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
. Inside the brown interval,
J_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
is constant
equal to
f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))
. On the pink interval,

J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
is constant equal to
f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k+1))
. On the intervals blue and yellow, the test function is a
convex combination of these two values, where the weights are given by
the function defined in (
7.7
). In particular, restricted to the
blue interval,
J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
is constant equal to

{\textsf{w}}_{k,k+1}^{1,2}\,f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k+1))+[1-{\textsf{w}}_{k,k+1}^{1,2}]\,f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))
; while on the yellow
interval, the test function is constant equal to

{\textsf{w}}_{k,k+1}^{2,3}\,f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k+1))+[1-{\textsf{w}}_{k,k+1}^{2,3}]\,f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))
.

The test function

For convenience, we first define the test function on
{\mathbb{R}}
, then we
project it onto the torus. We refer to figures
2
and
3
for the
notation introduced below. Fix an
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}-
equivalent class

{\mathfrak{D}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
. Let
{\mathscr{D}}\subset{\mathscr{S}}_{p}
be the

{\mathbb{X}}_{p}-
equivalence class, such that

\{\Pi({\mathscr{M}}):{\mathscr{M}}\in{\mathscr{D}}\}={\mathfrak{D}}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(0)\leq{\mathscr{M}}\leq{\mathscr{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{u}}_{p}-1)\quad\forall\;{\mathscr{M}}\in{\mathscr{D}}.

(7.2)

Let

D\subset\llbracket 0,\,{\mathfrak{u}}_{p}-1\rrbracket=\{0,\dots,{\mathfrak{u}}_{p}-1\}
be the set of indices associated to the states contained in

{\mathscr{D}}
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}:=\left\{k\in\llbracket 0,\dots,{\mathfrak{u}}_{p}-1\rrbracket:{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)\in{\mathscr{D}}\right\}\,=\,\left\{k\in\llbracket 0,\dots,{\mathfrak{u}}_{p}-1\rrbracket:{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)\in{\mathfrak{D}}\right\}
. Denote by
l_{{\mathfrak{D}}},r_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
the
leftmost, rightmost element of
D
, respectively,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}l_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}:=\min\{k\in D\}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}:=\max\{k\in D\}
. Hence,

D\,=\,\llbracket l_{{\mathfrak{D}}},\,r_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\rrbracket\,.

Recall the definition of the sets
{\mathscr{W}}_{k-1,k}^{(p)}
,
k\in S_{p}
,
introduced in (
2.16
). Let
{\mathscr{W}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
be the set of
absolute maxima separating the metastable states
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
,

k\in\llbracket l_{{\mathfrak{D}}}-1,\,r_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+1\rrbracket
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{W}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}\,:=\,\bigcup_{k\,=\,l_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}^{r_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+1}{\mathscr{W}}_{k-1,k}^{(p)}\,.

(7.3)

We construct the test function
J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
in several
steps. We start defining it at neighborhoods of each local maxima
belonging to
{\mathscr{W}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
. For every

l_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\leq k\leq r_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+1
, denote the maxima of

{\mathscr{W}}_{k-1,k}^{(p)}
by

\sigma^{1}_{k-1,k}<\dots<\sigma^{{\mathfrak{r}}_{k-1,k}}_{k-1,k};

(7.4)

where
{\mathfrak{r}}_{k-1,k}
is the number of elements of

{\mathscr{W}}_{k-1,k}^{(p)}
.

For
1\leq r\leq{\mathfrak{r}}_{k-1,k}
, denote by

{\mathscr{L}}_{k-1,k}^{r,\epsilon}
the linearization of the generator

{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}
around
\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r}
(centered at the origin
and acting on the space
C^{2}({\mathbb{R}}))
.

({\mathscr{L}}_{k-1,k}^{r,\epsilon}\psi)(x)\;=\;x\,{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r})\,\,\psi^{\prime}(x)\,+\,\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}(\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r})\,\psi^{\prime\prime}(x)\,.

Let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varrho_{k-1,k}^{r,\epsilon}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{[}}0,1]

be the solution of the Poisson equation

{\mathscr{L}}_{k-1,k}^{r,\epsilon}u=0,\;\;u(-\infty)=0,\;\;u(+\infty)=1.

Clearly,

\varrho_{k-1,k}^{r,\epsilon}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{-\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r})}{2\pi\epsilon}}\,\int_{-\infty}^{x}e^{S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r})y^{2}/2\epsilon}\;dy\,.

(7.5)

Denote by
\delta_{k-1,k}^{r}
the scale given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{k-1,k}^{r}}\,:=\,\delta_{k-1,k}^{r}(\epsilon)\,=\,\sqrt{\frac{2\,\epsilon\log\epsilon^{-1}}{-\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r})}}\;.

(7.6)

The next assertion states that the function

\varrho_{k-1,k}^{r,\epsilon}(\cdot)
at
\pm\,\delta_{k-1,k}^{r}
is
very close to its asymptotic values. The proof of the assertion relies
on elementary Gaussian bounds.

Assertion 7.2
.

There exists a finite constant
C_{0}
such that

\varrho_{k-1,k}^{r,\epsilon}(-\,\delta_{k-1,k}^{r})\,\leq\,C_{0}\,\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{\log\epsilon^{-1}}}\,,\quad\varrho_{k-1,k}^{r,\epsilon}(\delta_{k-1,k}^{r})\,\geq\,1\,-\,C_{0}\,\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{\log\epsilon^{-1}}}

for all
l_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\leq k\leq r_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+1
,

1\leq r\leq{\mathfrak{r}}_{k-1,k}
, and
\epsilon>0
.

Fix
l_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\leq k\leq r_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+1
. Denote by

{\textsf{w}}_{k-1,k}^{r,r+1}
,
0\leq r\leq{\mathfrak{r}}_{k-1,k}
, the
(asymptotic) probability that the diffusion
X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
,
starting from a point

x\in(\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r},\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r+1})
, hits

{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
before
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k-1)
. By equations (2.27), (2.28) and
Remark 3.4 in
[
27
]
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\textsf{w}}_{k-1,k}^{r,r+1}}\,:=\,\frac{\sum_{l=1}^{r}1/\sqrt{-S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k-1,k}^{l})}}{\sum_{l=1}^{{\mathfrak{r}}_{k-1,k}}1/\sqrt{-S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k-1,k}^{l})}}\,.

(7.7)

Clearly,
{\textsf{w}}_{k-1,k}^{0,1}=0
and

{\textsf{w}}_{k-1,k}^{{\mathfrak{r}},{\mathfrak{r}}+1}=1
, where
{\mathfrak{r}}={\mathfrak{r}}_{k-1,k}
.

Recall that
\omega(\,\cdot\,|\,{\mathfrak{D}})
represents the measure

\omega
conditioned to
{\mathfrak{D}}
, and
\nu^{p}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(\cdot)
the
stationary state of the Markov chain
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}
reflected at

{\mathfrak{D}}
. Lemma
B.1
provides an explicit formula for

\nu^{p}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(\cdot)
. Let
f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be
function given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}})}\,:=\,\sqrt{\frac{\omega({\mathcal{M}}|{\mathfrak{D}})}{\nu^{p}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}})}}\,1\{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathfrak{D}}\}\,.

(7.8)

Fix
l_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\leq k\leq r_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+1
,
1\leq r\leq{\mathfrak{r}}_{k-1,k}
,
and recall the weights
{\textsf{w}}_{k-1,k}^{r,r+1}
introduced in
(
7.7
). Let
f_{k-1,k}^{r,r+1}
be the asymptotic expectation of

f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(X_{\epsilon}(\tau))
, where
\tau
is the hitting time of the
set
\{{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k-1),{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)\}
, when the process starts from a
point between
\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r}
, and
\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r+1}
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f_{k-1,k}^{r,r+1}}\,:=\,{\textsf{w}}_{k-1,k}^{r,r+1}\,f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))\,+\,[\,1-{\textsf{w}}_{k-1,k}^{r,r+1}\,\,]\,f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k-1))\,.

(7.9)

Note that, by the definition of
f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(\cdot)
,

f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(l_{{\mathfrak{D}}}-1))=f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(r_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+1))=0
.

Denote by
\rho_{k-1,k}^{r,\epsilon}(x)
a function similar to

\varrho_{k-1,k}^{r,\epsilon}(x)
, but which interpolates between

f_{k-1,k}^{r-1,r}
and
f_{k-1,k}^{r,r+1}
instead of interpolating
between
0
and
1
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\rho_{k-1,k}^{r,\epsilon}(x)}:=f_{k-1,k}^{r,r+1}\,\varrho_{k-1,k}^{r,\epsilon}(x)\,+\,f_{k-1,k}^{r-1,r}\,[\,1-\varrho_{k-1,k}^{r,\epsilon}(x)\,]

(7.10)

We are now in a position to define the test function

J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}(\cdot)
. This is done writing the torus as the
union of disjoint sets and defining the test function in each of these
sets. We refer to figures
2
and
3
for an illustration of the
definitions. Below
k
is fixed and belongs to the interval
\llbracket\,l_{{\mathfrak{D}}},\,r_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+1\,\rrbracket
.

For
1\leq r\leq{\mathfrak{r}}_{k-1,k}
, let the scale
\eta_{k-1,k}^{r}
be
given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\eta_{k-1,k}^{r}}\,:=\,\frac{\epsilon}{\delta_{k-1,k}^{r}}\;=\;\sqrt{\frac{-\,\epsilon\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r})}{2\,\log\epsilon^{-1}}}\;.

Denote by
\sigma^{-}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
,
\sigma^{+}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
are the leftmost,
rightmost maxima in
{\mathscr{W}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
, respectively,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma^{-}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}\,:=\,\min\{\sigma\in{\mathscr{W}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\}\,,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma^{+}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}:=\max\{\sigma\in{\mathscr{W}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\}\,.

(7.11)

Thus,
\sigma^{-}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}=\sigma_{l_{{\mathfrak{D}}}-1,l_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}^{1}
,

\sigma^{+}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}=\sigma_{r_{{\mathfrak{D}}},r_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+1}^{{\mathfrak{r}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}
, where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{r}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}:={\mathfrak{r}}_{r_{{\mathfrak{D}}},r_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+1}
, are
respectively the leftmost, rightmost global maxima separating the sets

\{{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k):k\in D\}
and
\{{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k):k\not\in D\}
. Denote
by
\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
the neighborhood of
{\mathfrak{D}}
defined by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}\,:=\,(\sigma^{-}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}-\delta_{l_{{\mathfrak{D}}}-1,l_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}^{1}-\eta_{l_{{\mathfrak{D}}}-1,l_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}^{1}\,,\,\sigma^{+}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+\delta_{r_{{\mathfrak{D}}},r_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+1}^{{\mathfrak{r}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}+\eta_{r_{{\mathfrak{D}}},r_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+1}^{{\mathfrak{r}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}})\,.

(7.12)

Set the test function to vanish outside the interval
\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
:

J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}(x)=0\,,\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\,.

Inside the set
\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
, we first define the test function
close to the global maxima
\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r}
and far from
them. Then, we interpolate smoothly between these intervals. For

1\leq r\leq{\mathfrak{r}}_{k-1,k}
, let
\Lambda_{k-1,k}^{r}
be the

\delta_{k-1,k}^{r}
-neighborhood of
\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Lambda_{k-1,k}^{r}}\,:=\,\big[\,\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r}-\delta_{k-1,k}^{r}\,,\,\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r}+\delta_{k-1,k}^{r}\,\big]\;.

(7.13)

The test function
J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
is defined on

\Lambda_{k-1,k}^{r}
as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}(x)}\,:=\,\rho_{k-1,k}^{r,\epsilon}(x-\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r})\,,\quad x\in\Lambda_{k-1,k}^{r}\,.

(7.14)

Let
C_{k-1,k}^{r,r+1}
be the interval between successive
maxima given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{k-1,k}^{r,r+1}}\,:=\,\big[\,\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r}+\delta_{k-1,k}^{r}+\eta_{k-1,k}^{r}\,,\,\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r+1}-\delta_{k-1,k}^{r+1}-\eta_{k-1,k}^{r+1}\,\big]\;.

(7.15)

In the interval
C_{k-1,k}^{r,r+1}
the test function

J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}(\cdot)
is constant and takes the value

f_{k-1,k}^{r,r+1}
introduced in (
7.9
):

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}(x)}\,:=\,f_{k-1,k}^{r,r+1}\,.

(7.16)

In particular,
J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}(x)=f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))
for

x\in C_{k-1,k}^{{\mathfrak{r}},{\mathfrak{r}}+1}
, where
{\mathfrak{r}}={\mathfrak{r}}_{k-1,k}
,
and
J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}(x)=f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k-1))
for

x\in C_{k-1,k}^{0,1}
.

To complete the definition of
J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
, consider the
binding sets
B_{k-1,k}^{r,-}

B_{k-1,k}^{r,+}
,
B_{k-1,k}^{r}
,

B_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
given by

\begin{gathered}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}B_{k-1,k}^{r,-}}\,:=\,(\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r}-\delta_{k-1,k}^{r}-\eta_{k-1,k}^{r}\,,\,\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r}-\delta_{k-1,k}^{r})\;,\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}B_{k-1,k}^{r,+}}\,:=\,(\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r}+\delta_{k-1,k}^{r}\,,\,\sigma_{k-1,k}^{r}+\delta_{k-1,k}^{r}+\eta_{k-1,k}^{r})\;,\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}B_{k-1,k}^{r}}:=B_{k-1,k}^{r,-}\cup B_{k-1,k}^{r,+}\,,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}B_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}\,:=\,\bigcup_{k=l_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}^{r_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+1}\bigcup_{r=1}^{{\mathfrak{r}}_{k-1,1}}B_{k-1,k}^{r}\,.\end{gathered}

(7.17)

On these biding sets, let
J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
be defined as a smooth
function
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Phi_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}}
in
C^{2}(B_{{\mathfrak{D}}})

that satisfies the following properties

•

\Phi_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
turns
J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
into an
element in
C^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
,

•

\Phi_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}^{\prime}(x)\neq 0
for every
x\in B_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
.

•

\sup_{x\in B_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}|\Phi_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}^{\prime}(x)|=O(\sqrt{\epsilon})
.

Assertion
7.2
guarantees the existence of
such a function since the length of each interval
B_{k-1,k}^{r,\pm}

is of order
\eta_{k-1,k}^{r}
.

Recall from (
7.12
) the definition of the interval

\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
. Since there is more than one
{\mathbb{X}}_{p}
-equivalent
class, there exist
a>0
and
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that

\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\subset(\sigma^{-}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}-a,\sigma^{-}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}-a+1)
for all
0<\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}
. Thus,
\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
can be
considered as a subset of the torus, and
J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
as a
function on the torus of class
C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
.

The local Gibbs measure

In this subsection,
{\mathfrak{D}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
is a

\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}-
equivalence class and
{\mathscr{D}}
its lifted version
to
{\mathbb{R}}
satisfying (
7.2
). Recall the notation
introduced in (
7.12
). Fix a positive number
a>0
sufficiently
small such that

\big(\,\sigma^{-}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}-a\,,\,\sigma^{-}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\,\big)\,\cap{\mathscr{M}}\,=\,\varnothing\,,\quad\big(\,\sigma^{+}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\,,\,\sigma^{+}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+a\,\big)\,\cap\,{\mathscr{M}}=\varnothing\,.

Clearly,

\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\subset(\sigma^{-}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}-a,\sigma^{+}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+a)

for
\epsilon
sufficiently small. Let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}\colon{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a function of class

C^{2}({\mathbb{T}})
such that

S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(\Pi(x))\,=\,S(x)\;,\quad x\,\in\,(\sigma^{-}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}-a\,,\,\sigma^{+}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+a)\,.

From now on, we work on the torus. Denote by

{\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}})\subset{\mathcal{M}}_{p}
the set of local minima of level
p

belonging to
{\mathfrak{D}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}})}:=\bigcup_{{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathfrak{D}}}{\mathcal{M}}\,,

and by
{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))\subset{\mathbb{T}}
the union of the
associated metastable wells,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))}:=\bigcup_{m\in{\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}})}{\mathcal{E}}(m)\,.

The next result follows directly from Proposition

A.5
, and the definitions of the sets

\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
,
{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))
.

Proposition 7.3
.

For every
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}(\cdot)-
equivalence class

{\mathfrak{D}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
,

\displaystyle\min\big\{\,S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(m)\;:\;m\in\big[\,\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\cap{\mathcal{M}}\,\big]\setminus{\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}})\,\big\}\;>\;S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))\,,

\displaystyle\inf_{x\,\in\,\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\setminus{\mathscr{E}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))}S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(x)\,>\,S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))\,.

Recall the definition of the weights
\pi_{1}(j)
,
\pi_{p}(k)
,

j\in S_{1}
,
k\in S_{p}
, introduced in (
2.11
), (
2.21
),
respectively.

Corollary 7.4
.

Fix a
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}(\cdot)-
equivalence class

{\mathfrak{D}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
. For every continuous function

\phi\in C({\mathbb{T}})
,

\int_{\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}\frac{\phi(x)}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,e^{-S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(x)/\epsilon}\,dx\,=\,[1+O(\epsilon)]\,\sqrt{\epsilon}\,e^{-S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))/\epsilon}\,\sum_{j:m_{j}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}})}\phi(m_{j})\pi_{1}(j)\,.

In particular,

\int_{\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,e^{-S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(x)/\epsilon}dx\,=\,[1+O(\epsilon)]\,\sqrt{\epsilon}\,e^{-S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))/\epsilon}\,\sum_{k\in D}\pi_{p}(k)\,.

Proof.

Fix
\phi\in C({\mathbb{T}})
, and write

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}}\frac{\phi(x)}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,e^{-S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(x)/\epsilon}\,dx\,=\,\int_{{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))}\frac{\phi(x)}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,e^{-S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(x)/\epsilon}\,dx\,+\,\int_{\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\setminus{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))}\frac{\phi(x)}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,e^{-S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(x)/\epsilon}\,dx\,.

We treat each integral on the right-hand side separately. Since
\phi

is bounded and
a
is bounded below by a strictly positive
constant, the second integral is less than or equal to

\displaystyle C_{0}\,e^{-S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))/\epsilon}\sup_{x\in\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\setminus{\mathscr{E}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))}\,e^{\,[S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))-S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(x)]\,/\,\epsilon}

for some finite constant
C_{0}
. By Proposition
7.3
, this
expression is bounded by

\displaystyle e^{-\,[\,S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))\,+\,h^{\prime}\,]\,/\,\epsilon}\,,

for some positive constant
h^{\prime}>0
.

We turn to the first integral. As
\phi(\cdot)
, and
{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)

are continuous functions, by the definition of
{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))

and Laplace’s method, the first integral is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in{\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}})}\int_{{\mathcal{E}}(m)}\frac{\phi(x)}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}e^{-S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(x)/\epsilon}\,dx

\displaystyle=\,e^{-S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))/\epsilon}\sum_{m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}})}\frac{\phi(m)}{{\textsf{a}}(m)}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi\epsilon}{S^{\prime\prime}(m)}}\left(1+O(\epsilon)\right)

\displaystyle=\,(1+O(\epsilon))\,\sqrt{\epsilon}\,e^{-S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))/\epsilon}\sum_{k\in D}\sum_{m\in{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)}\phi(m)\pi_{1}(k).

To conclude the proof, in remains to recollect the previous estimates,
and recall the definition of
\pi_{p}(k)
given in (
2.21
).
∎

A recovery sequence
\mu_{\epsilon}
for
\mu

In this subsection we construct a recovery sequence
\mu_{\epsilon}
for
the measure
\mu
. Fix a
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}(\cdot)-
equivalence class

{\mathfrak{D}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
, and let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mu_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
be the
probability measure given by the density

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}(x)}\,:=\,\frac{\big[J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}(x)\big]^{2}\,e^{-S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(x)/\epsilon}}{{\textsf{a}}(x)\,c_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(\epsilon)}\,,

(7.18)

where
J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
and
S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
are the
functions defined in the previous subsection, and

c_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(\epsilon)
is a normalization constant that turns

\mu_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
into a probability measure.

Recall the definition of the measure
\mu
introduced in
(
7.1
) and that we assumed
\omega
to be strictly
positive. Thus, with the notation introduced in Section

5
,
{\mathcal{S}}^{(\omega)}_{p}={\mathcal{S}}_{p}
and the sets
{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}^{p}
,

1\leq r\leq l_{p}
, are exactly the

\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}(\cdot)-
equivalent classes with two or more
elements. Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}k_{p}}\geq 0
be the number of

\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}-
equivalent classes having exactly one element, and
denote them by
{\mathfrak{C}}_{r}
,
1\leq r\leq k_{p}
. Define

\mu_{\epsilon}\in{\textsf{P}}({\mathbb{T}})
as the convex combination, with respect
to
\omega
, of the measures

\{\mu_{{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}}\}_{r=1}^{l_{p}}\cup\{\mu_{{\mathfrak{C}}_{r}}\}_{r=1}^{k_{p}}
:

\mu_{\epsilon}:=\sum_{r=1}^{l_{p}}\omega({\mathfrak{D}}_{r})\,\mu_{{\mathfrak{D}}_{r},\epsilon}\,+\,\sum_{r=1}^{k_{p}}\omega({\mathfrak{C}}_{r})\,\mu_{{\mathfrak{C}}_{r},\epsilon}\,.

(7.19)

Assertion 7.5
.

For every
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}(\cdot)-
equivalent class
{\mathfrak{D}}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\,e^{S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))/\epsilon}\,c_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(\epsilon)\,\,=\,\sum_{k\in D}\pi_{p}(k)\,.

Proof.

Fix a
\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}(\cdot)-
equivalence class
{\mathfrak{D}}
. By the definitions of

c_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(\epsilon)
,
\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
, and
J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
,

\displaystyle c_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(\epsilon)=\sum_{k\in D}f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))^{2}\,\int_{{\mathcal{E}}_{p}(k)}\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,e^{-S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(x)/\epsilon}\,dx+\int_{\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}}\setminus{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))}\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\big[J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}(x)\big]^{2}\,e^{-S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(x)/\epsilon}\,dx\,.

We treat each integral separately. Since
J_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon}
is
uniformy bounded, by the first part of the proof of Corollary

7.4
, the second integral is bounded above by

e^{-[\,S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))\,+\,h^{\prime}\,]\,/\,\epsilon}
, for some
positive constant
h^{\prime}>0
.

We turn to the first integral. By the proof of Corollary

7.4
, this integral is equal to

\displaystyle[\,1+O(\epsilon)\,]\,e^{-S_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}({\mathfrak{D}}))/\epsilon}\,\sqrt{\epsilon}\,\sum_{k\in D}f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))^{2}\,\pi_{p}(k)\,.

To complete the proof, it remains to recall the definitions of

f_{{\mathfrak{D}}}
and
\nu_{{\mathfrak{D}}}^{p}
, given in (
7.8
) and Lemma

B.1
, respectively; and to recollect the previous
estimates.
∎

Lemma 7.6
.

The sequence
\{\mu_{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon>0}
, given by
(
7.19
), weakly converges to
\mu
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

Proof.

Fix
f\in C({\mathbb{T}})
. By the definition of
\mu_{\epsilon}
,

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}f(x)\;d\mu_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{l_{p}}\omega({\mathfrak{D}}_{r})\,\frac{1}{c_{{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}}(\epsilon)}\,\int_{\Omega_{{\mathfrak{D}}_{r}}}\;f(x)\;J_{{\mathfrak{D}}_{r},\epsilon}(x)^{2}\;\frac{e^{-S_{{\mathfrak{D}}r}(x)/\epsilon}}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\;dx

\displaystyle+\sum_{l=1}^{k_{p}}\omega({\mathfrak{C}}_{l})\,\frac{1}{c_{{\mathfrak{C}}_{l}}(\epsilon)}\int_{\Omega_{{\mathfrak{C}}_{l}}}f(x)\;J_{{\mathfrak{C}}_{l},\epsilon}(x)^{2}\;\frac{e^{-S_{{\mathfrak{C}}_{l}}(x)/\epsilon}}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\;dx.

A calculation, similar to the one performed in the proof of Assertion

7.5
, yields that this sum is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{l_{p}}\sum_{k\in D_{r}}\omega({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))\,\frac{1}{\pi_{1}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))}\,\sum_{{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)}f({\mathfrak{m}})\,\pi_{1}(\{{\mathfrak{m}}\})

\displaystyle+\sum_{r=1}^{k_{p}}\sum_{k\in C_{r}}\omega({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))\,\frac{1}{\pi_{1}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k))}\,\sum_{{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)}f({\mathfrak{m}})\,\pi_{1}(\{{\mathfrak{m}}\})+r(\epsilon)\,,

where
r(\epsilon)
is an error that vanishes as
\epsilon\to 0
. By
(
7.1
) and the expression (
2.25
) for the measure

\mu^{p}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(k)}
, this expression is equal to

\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}f(x)\;d\mu+r(\epsilon)
, which completes the proof
of the lemma.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.1

The following lemma, whose proof is postponed to the next subsection,
collects the required asymptotic bounds to establish the proposition.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
\mu_{\epsilon}
be the sequence defined in
(
7.19
). Fix a

\widehat{{\mathbb{X}}}_{p}(\cdot)-
equivalence class
{\mathfrak{D}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
.

(1)

If
{\mathfrak{D}}
has two or more elements, then

\displaystyle\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon})\,\leq\,{\mathbb{I}}_{{\mathfrak{D}}}^{(p)}(\omega(\,\cdot\,|\,{\mathfrak{D}}))

\displaystyle\quad\,+\,\omega({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(l_{{\mathfrak{D}}})\,|\,{\mathfrak{D}})\,R_{p}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(l_{{\mathfrak{D}}})\,,{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(l_{{\mathfrak{D}}}-1))\,+\,\omega({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(r_{{\mathfrak{D}}})\,|\,{\mathfrak{D}})\,R_{p}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(r_{{\mathfrak{D}}})\,,{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(r_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+1))\,.

(2)

If
{\mathfrak{D}}
is a singleton and its element is a transient state, then

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}\,{\mathscr{I}}_{\epsilon}(\mu_{{\mathfrak{D}},\epsilon})\,\leq\,R_{p}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(l_{{\mathfrak{D}}})\,,{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(l_{{\mathfrak{D}}}-1))\,+\,R_{p}({\mathcal{M}}_{p}(r_{{\mathfrak{D}}})\,,{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(r_{{\mathfrak{D}}}+1))\,.

(3)

If
{\mathfrak{D}}
is a singleton and its element is an absorbing state,
then
